4.1.2 RESILIENCY MARKET ECONOMIC MODELS 

  CONGESTED CONSTRAINTS  

SPP identified resiliency needs based on congested constraints using the RMEMs. To identify a 
list of congested constraints, the standard MEM analysis methodology described in section 4.1 
of the ITP Manual was applied to the RMEMs. As the RMEMs are only one-month simulations, a 
proportional congestion threshold was calculated by dividing the annual flowgate congestion 
score of $50,000/MW by 12. SPP used the resulting monthly flowgate congestion score of 
$4,246/MW to identify needs in this assessment. To analyze the southeastern SPP target area, 
SPP applied a proportional $1,667/MW threshold.  

There were 652 unique economic needs (monitored-contingent element pairs) in the 2025 ITP. 
SPP observed the largest congestion scores in these three SPP areas: Tulsa, OK (GRDA), New 
Mexico (SPS), and southeastern SPP footprint target area (AEP). This was consistent with the 
most congested areas identified in the MEMs.  

Congestion was especially aggressive because of the resiliency conditions applied to the RMEM 
models. In the summer models, this included extreme summer weather conditions such as 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

103 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

effects of prolonged high temperatures, droughts, low wind and generation maintenance 
outages. In the winter models, this included effects of extreme cold and duration such as 
increased load, capacity reductions or temperature related outages and limited fuel availability. 
In the Future 2 models, there was increased load from higher-than-expected electric vehicle 
(EV) adoption and load growth (including large spot loads). A high number of monitored 
constraints contributed to a significant number of economic needs overall.  

While not the focus of the ITP, some facilities outside of the SPP footprint also observed high 
congestion scores. Like non-resiliency market economic models, to be identified as a need, 
external facilities must meet the congestion score threshold and provide at least $1 million in 
potential benefit to SPP’s region. Other notable congestion was observed in southeastern New 
Mexico, in the Texas panhandle and in northeastern Oklahoma.

4.2  RELIABILITY NEEDS 

4.2.1 BASE RELIABILITY NEEDS ASSESSMENT 

Contingency analysis for the BR models consisted of analyzing P0, P1 and P2.1 planning events 
from Table 1 in the NERC TPL-001 standard, as well as remaining events that do not allow for 
non-consequential load loss or the interruption of firm transmission service. Because the 2025 
ITP’s NERC TPL-001 P3 planning event analysis identified an exceptionally large number of 
transmission needs, SPP limited reporting to the 10 worst violations per monitored element in 
each model. This approach aligns with SPP’s existing practice for publishing P6 event violations 
under the NERC TPL compliance assessment.  

During the needs assessment, potential violations were solved or marked invalid through 
methods such as reactive device setting adjustments, model updates, and identification of 
invalid contingencies, non-load-serving buses and facilities not under SPP’s functional control. 
Preliminary violations were posted ahead of the needs assessment to provide TOs with the 
opportunity to review the violations and provide invalidation feedback prior to the posting of 
the needs and opening of the detailed project proposals (DPP) window. Stakeholder feedback 
improved the quality of the final list of identified needs, helped staff remove invalid needs and 
improved the pertinence of DPPs submitted by stakeholders.  

Figure 4.4 and Figure 4.5 summarize the final quantity of thermal and voltage needs that were 
unable to be mitigated during the screening process. Figure 4.6 and Figure 4.7 show their 
locations.

4.2.2 NON-CONVERGED CONTINGENCY CASES 

SPP used engineering judgment to resolve non-converged cases from the contingency analysis. 
All non-converged cases were resolved either through alternate powerflow solve 
methodologies, model corrections, or the contingencies were determined to be invalid. Voltage 
collapse was identified in all BR cases. The two main areas where voltage collapse occurred 
were southern New Mexico and Oklahoma around Texas Border.  

4.2.3 SHORT-CIRCUIT ASSESSMENT 

SPP provided the total bus fault current study results for single-line-to-ground (SLG) and three-
phase faults to transmission planners (TPs) for review.  

TPs were required to evaluate the results and indicate if any fault-interrupting equipment 
would have its duty ratings exceeded by the maximum available fault current. For equipment 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

108 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

that would have its duty ratings exceeded, the TP provided the applicable duty rating of the 
equipment, and SPP identified the violation as a short-circuit need.  

While abiding by the requirements of TPL-001, the TPs could have performed their own short-
circuit analysis to identify corrective actions plans. However, any corrective action plans that 
result in the recommended issuance of an NTC are based on the SPP short-circuit analysis.  

The TPs that identified short-circuit needs were Evergy and Liberty Utilities. The needs are 
depicted in Figure 4.8.

4.3  PUBLIC POLICY NEEDS 

Policy needs are identified by evaluating the curtailment in renewable energy generation, which 
may prevent utilities from meeting their energy-based RPS. Each region's renewable energy 
targets were assessed at a utility-specific level to determine compliance with these mandates or 
goals. Policy needs arise from the inability to deploy renewable generation due to congestion, 
impacting the utilities' ability to fulfill their state-specific renewable goals or mandates. In the 

2025 ITP Assessment Report

2025 ITP, all utilities successfully achieved their renewable targets, resulting in no identified 
policy needs.

intervals or if it experienced at least $10 million in congestion costs over the previous 24 
months.

least 20% of the previous 24 months, either in a breached or binding state in the real-time 
balancing market.

previous 24 months and two facilities with a congestion cost totaling more than $50 million

Table 4.5: Reliability Operational Needs 

New criteria for identifying persistent operational needs were introduced in the 2024 ITP. The 
new criteria were a result of a revision to the ITP Manual that aimed to align criteria with 
portions of the SCRIPT T3 recommendations. The objective of the recommendation was to 
clarify which SPP flowgates would be included in the list of flowgates and to classify facilities as 
economic needs or reliability needs. Facilities that experienced congestion due to planned or 
forced historical outages would be classified as economic needs. Facilities where pre-
contingency or post-contingency facility ratings or voltage exceedances were experienced in 
real-time operations would be classified as reliability needs. 

The identification of these real time events improves system flexibility by addressing 
operational issues that can enhance the flexibility of the transmission system. This is crucial in 
accommodating changing energy demands, integrating new generation sources, and 
supporting emerging technologies such as energy storage. A more flexible approach can adapt 
to evolving needs and reduce the need for costly infrastructure investments. It will also enhance 
resiliency using persistent operational events that undermine the robustness. 

SPP identified 150 facilities as operational reliability needs based on System Operating Limit 
exceedances that have occurred in real-time operations where the total cumulative time 
exceeded four days over the previous 24 months. Of the 150 facilities, 139 were voltage 
exceedances and the remaining 11 were thermal exceedances. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report

Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

4.5  RESILIENCY NEEDS ASSESSMENT 

For 2025 ITP, SPP and stakeholders created new resiliency need types to support development 
of solutions that best address resiliency conditions. For example, a project created to address 
economic issues may not be best evaluated under reliability or resiliency standards. To 
understand the merits of projects that best support resiliency, new resiliency needs had to be 
defined as part of the needs assessment. With new resiliency needs, solutions could be 
developed to directly address the needs to be fairly evaluated by their performance in resiliency 
models. 

Included as a new guidepost for building resiliency driven solutions are three new resiliency 
needs categories: addressing peak LMP during resiliency hours, considering sub-regional 
transferability, and load shed reduction. LMP reduction utilized the RMEMs, while the other two 
types of needs utilized the RPMs. 

4.5.1 LOCATIONAL MARGINAL PRICE  

  DURING RESILIENCY HOURS  

A key economic driver during historical peak events is demand outpacing available and 
deliverable generation, driving LMP prices up. At the same time, numerous resources face 
curtailment due to transmission constraints. Similar to non-peak times, improving a generator’s 
ability to deliver energy to the system via transmission upgrades during peak events would 
offset higher than normal LMP rates.  

To build these economically driven needs, SPP started by finding a three-day window in the 
RMEMs with the highest average LMP. Filtering down the LMP hours, SPP looked at all 
generators that were curtailed during that same window. Figure 4.9 shows this process.

4.5.2 TRANSFERABILITY  

  ANALYSIS 

Another key indicator for system performance during stressed conditions is the ability for sub-
regions to transfer large amounts of power. The ITP usually considers how power delivery may 
be limited, but not to the extent of finding the limits at the subregional level.  

SPP and stakeholders agreed to use LOLE zones to act as the six subregional areas to perform 
the transferability analysis (See Figure 4.12). Using the RPMs, each zone’s generation output 
was reduced, while neighboring zones’ generation output was increased to balance out the 
demand. The generation was reduced (and increased) incrementally until voltage collapsed to 
simulate the transfer. The amount of generation transferred, in MW, before voltage collapsed, 
defined the transfer capability into the zone.  

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

115 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

Figure 4.12: LOLE Zone Map 

Next, SPP and stakeholders determined a reasonable or expected amount of transferability for 
each zone. The PRM is a required amount of excess capacity in MW that each LRE is responsible 
for maintaining for the purpose of reserves. Because the PRM represents the required reserve 
margin that must be available for sharing across the BA, it was used as the metric to establish 
zone-level transferability targets. The end goal was evaluating whether each subregion is 
capable of sharing their resources for a more flexible system during emergency events. MW 
targets were calculated by subregion using 2025 ITP load review data.  

The target calculated using the PRM was compared to the transfer capability. If the transfer 
capability was less than the calculated PRM target, then a SPP defined that transfer scenario as 
a need, given in MW.  

Following the initial analysis, SPP opted to also do the transfer analysis in reverse, meaning 
generation in the zone was ramped up while generation in neighboring zones was ramped 
down. This allowed SPP to compare the bi-directional power flow during emergency events. It 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

116 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

also reduced the impact of the initial generation dispatch in the models and gives a clearer 
picture of true transfer capability and transmission constraints.  

Figure 4.13: Subregional Inflow, Outflow Transferability Test Procedures 

Once SPP had a list of transferability limitations between subregional zones, contingency 
analysis was performed to highlight the value of specific flowgates between subregions. This 
informs SPP and stakeholders of potential priority points and aids in the creation of solutions. 

  RESULTS  

Transfer analysis was performed on eight different models (year five and year 10 RPM models) 
and six LOLE zones with each zone having two different transfer scenarios analyzed. This 
resulted in a total of 96 transfer scenarios that were evaluated. Of those 96 scenarios, 49 were 
determined to have transfer needs based on the PRM MW requirements. Voltage and thermal 
violations associated with each need and each applicable contingency, were also posted to aid 
in solution development. Of the needs posted, LOLE zone six had the largest transfer needs in 
MW. SPP staff observed voltage instability in the SPS area that caused early voltage collapse for 
some transfer scenarios outside of the SPS area. During portfolio development staff re-assessed 
the needs after the SPS area instability was addressed. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report

Southwest Power Pool, Inc.

4.5.3 LOAD SHED  

  EVALUATION  

Load shed traditionally happens when transmissions assets are overloaded, forecasted to be 
overloaded, when voltage cannot be maintained, or when voltage is forecasted to be outside of 
NERC or Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) requirements. Broadly, isolated pockets 
of directed load shed have happened under such conditions. Winter Storm Uri provided an 
opportunity to understand how operational large scale load shed was required.  

SPP considered shedding load where thermal or voltage violations are indicated in the planning 
models. This methodology breaks down into two steps – cascading re-dispatch and security 
constrained re-dispatch.  

The cascading load shed model works to understand how shedding load at one step reduces 
overloads or voltage violations elsewhere under the assumption that not all load needs to be 
shed to address various thermal or voltage violations. It’s a process that first targets lower node 
voltages, anything below 0.8 p.u and sheds the load. Once the model is re-run, any node with 
voltages below 0.85 p.u. were then shed. This process iterated at 0.9 p.u. and 0.95 p.u. until all 
nodes were shed to maintain voltage above 0.95 p.u. Cascading delivers higher estimated load 
shed but eliminates nearly all remaining voltage and thermal violations.  

The SCRD model, Security Constrained Re-Dispatch, is a more simplified process of evaluating 
potential load shed. SCRD considers thermal violations and works to redispatch around the 
thermal violations. Where thermal violations can’t be resolved through redispatch, load is shed 
at various nodes responsible. SCRD does not reveal the same level of load shed as cascading 
evaluation does, providing a more conservative load shed number.  

  RESULTS  

Performing the evaluation meant looking at the BR models and the RPMs and finding the 
difference between the two. Because the load forecast even in the BR models is so extreme, 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

118 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

there still exists the potential for load shed even in the BR. It’s important to assign needs to 
load shed only above and beyond the BR model indicated load shed.  

Figure 4.14: Resiliency Load Shed Needs Map 

Load shed from SCRD peaked at just over 3,500 MW for Future 2 and only 1,800 MW for Future 
1. Several hundred voltage violations remained, though nearly all thermal violations were 
resolved.  

The cascading load shed process produced much higher MW load shed amounts, while 
reduced voltage and thermal violations to zero. It is important to note 79% of load shed was 
between 0.9 p.u. and 0.95 p.u. Cascading output to resolve voltage violations up to 0.9 p.u. 
ranged from 1,700 MW in Future 1 to 4,300 MW in Future 2. Those numbers climbed to 7,400 
MW and nearly 15,000 MW respectively to resolve up to 0.95 p.u. Because of this, SPP 
considered only load shed needs of 0.9 p.u. and below.  

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

119 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc.

project, the year five models would not have solved or produced usable results for transmission 
evaluation. To enable contingency analysis, staff added fictitious reactive support in the area.

5 TARGET AREA: SOUTHEASTERN SPP 

FOOTPRINT 

During the 2025 ITP Scope development milestone, AEP sought approval from the TWG and 
ESWG to assess the southeastern SPP footprint in a target area study in the 2025 ITP. Target 
areas are typically designated when economic congestion coincides with other transmission 
system needs. This particular region has limited transmission capacity into the load pocket, 
making it heavily reliant on local generation. During generation or transmission outages, the 
area faces heightened risks of system instability, thermal and voltage violations, and increased 
economic congestion – resulting in a divergence in LMPs between the target area and the rest 
of the SPP system. The purpose of this target area study was to address reliability, operational, 
and economic needs that may not be adequately and comprehensively addressed through the 
standard ITP process.  

The TWG and ESWG granted approval for the target area study at the October 2024 joint TWG 
and ESWG. The purpose of this target area study was to address reliability, operational, and 
economic needs that may not be adequately and comprehensively addressed through the 
standard ITP process.  

AEP identified the following concerns to be addressed within the target area study:  

•  Operational issues  
•  Persistent low voltage during shoulder season in the AEP SWEPCO region contributing 

to periods of load shed in 2025 

•  Generation redispatch and deferment of planned outages to manage grid conditions 
•  PSO-SWEPCO interface limitations  
•  Economic issues  
•  LMP separation  
•  Energy curtailment and congestion costs  
•  Stability issues  
•  Generator instability and voltage collapse observed for P3, P4, P5, and P6 planning 

events, and extreme events  

•  Generation headroom, reactive support and inertia declining  
•  Unstable simulations in southern SWEPCO driven by the loss of one or more 345 kV 

source in the region from the west 

In addition to AEP’s concerns, SPP has noted increased flows from AEP to other areas 
experiencing shortfall. TWG and ESWG agreed that this study would utilize the 2025 ITP BR, 
MEM and RMEM models. The 2025 ITP scope also specified that “SPP may perform dynamic 
stability analysis using the most current MDAG dynamic models to evaluate the ability of 
recommended solutions to mitigate transient stability issues. “ 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

123 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

It was agreed that SPP staff assess contingencies with the ITP manual to monitor bus voltages 
and branch loadings. The resulting needs were posted in the 2025 ITP Needs Assessment 
posting.  

To support the economic analysis, SPP collaborated with AEP to identify constraints to include 
in the economic event files that go beyond the scope of the constraints traditionally identified 
by the constraint assessment. By including these additional constraints, SPP and AEP more 
accurately assessed the congestion in the southeastern SPP footprint.  

Since the identification of the target area in 2023, SPP has issued load shed orders within this 
load pocket. These events were driven by the risk of voltage collapse due to the unavailability 
of key transmission elements (including the Longwood–Sarepta 345 kV line), generator outages 
for seasonal maintenance, higher-than-forecasted demand, and insufficient reactive power 
support. While SPP has made improvements to its real-time study processes, infrastructure 
investments are still needed to increase energy import capability and enhance reliability in the 
area. 

SPP identified needs based on analysis that incorporated the increased focus on the 
southeastern SPP target area and identified optimal solutions to address these needs. 

5.1  NEEDS ASSESSMENT 

5.1.1  RELIABILITY NEEDS ASSESSMENT 

SPP performed the standard BR needs assessment process for the target area. 

5.1.2  ECONOMIC NEEDS ASSESSMENT 

SPP collaborated with AEP to identify additional constraints to include in the economic event 
files that go beyond the scope of the constraints traditionally identified by the constraint 
assessment. By including these additional constraints, SPP and AEP more accurately assessed 
the congestion in the southeastern SPP footprint. 

This target area includes the PSOSWEPCOTIES interface. An interface is a set of transmission 
lines that are monitored together instead of individually to protect the system. Interfaces 
usually are only monitored for system intact conditions and have a defined rating that is a 
proxy for a unique condition such as: contractual limit, transient stability limit, or voltage 
stability limit.  

SPP’s standard interface constraints are normally only enforced under base case conditions, 
however, AEP monitors secondary versions of the PSOSWEPCOTIES interface under certain N-1 
conditions with alternate ratings. Through collaboration with AEP, SPP staff included additional 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

124 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

interface constraints in the event file to more effectively identify economic congestion in this 
area.  

SPP identified needs based on analysis that incorporates the increased focus on the 
southeastern SPP target area. Economic need identification was based on a lower congestion 
threshold of $20,000/MW for the MEM, to ensure economic needs in the area were thoroughly 
captured. This value is reduced from the standard need thresholds of $50,000/MW.  

This analysis resulted in the identification of 14 unique constraints experiencing economic 
congestion in the MEMs within the Target Area. 

Figure 5.1: 2025 ITP Target Area in the Southeastern SPP Footprint and PSOSWEPCOTIES Interface 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

125 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

5.1.3  RESILIENCY NEEDS ASSESSMENT 

  ECONOMIC – RMEM 

Consistent with the economic analysis performed on the MEMs in the target area, SPP 
performed analysis on the RMEMs as well, using the additional interface constraints and 
reduced congestion threshold for need identification.  

Within the one-month RMEMs, economic need identification was based on a lower congestion 
threshold of $1,667/MW for the RMEM assessments, to ensure economic needs in the area 
were thoroughly captured. This value is reduced from the standard need threshold $4,667/MW. 
The discrepancy between the MEM and RMEM thresholds exists because the MEM is a 12-
month simulation and the RMEM is a one-month simulation. 

This analysis resulted in the identification of 15 unique constraints experiencing economic 
congestion in the RMEMs within the target area. 

  TRANSFERABILITY 

In addition to the standard needs assessment, staff also conducted a resiliency needs 
assessment to evaluate transferability between LOLE zones. The target area is primarily located 
within LOLE Zone six, which also showed the highest need for MW transfer of any individual 
LOLE zone. A more targeted study was also performed to help rank key projects within the 
target area.  

  LOAD SHED 

The target area was included in the system wide load shed assessment with load shed in the 
target area between 70 to 250 MW. Load shed within the SPP footprint in 2025 acted as an 
important point for validating the load shed analysis methodology. 

5.2  STABILITY ASSESSMENT 

Stability needs within the target area were identified as informational needs. To effectively 
evaluate and develop solutions for the target area, it was essential to clearly understand the 
relationships among identified needs and their correlation to stability-related informational 
requirements. SPP assessed various projects against the posted stability needs to ensure the 
needs were addressed and also to help rank projects.  

5.3  PORTFOLIO DEVELOPMENT 

To address the needs in the target area, SPP applied an integrated approach that considered 
resiliency, operational performance, transient stability, and economic congestion relief. This 
framework ensured that potential upgrades were evaluated not just for reliability, but also for 
their ability to deliver lasting system value. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

126 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

For resiliency, projects were assessed on their ability to support transfer capability, reduce load 
shed risk, and minimize adverse LMP impacts during stressed conditions. A key factor in this 
assessment was the inclusion of Future 2 spot loads, which introduced sudden injections of 
demand that solutions must withstand. The 2026 spot loads added 1,000 MW in the year five 
and year 10 MEM models and confirmed more than 500 MW for the 2026 BR models—all 
within the target area. In the 2025 MEM models, 650 MW and 1,000 MW were added in year 
five and year 10 respectively. These spot loads are rapidly approaching, and SPP anticipates 
further growth in coming years, underscoring the need for robust upgrades. 

From an operational perspective, SPP focused on modeling the February 19 and April 26 load 
shed events in the target area. These case studies provided insight into how proposed solutions 
could have changed the outcomes of past events by mitigating the conditions that led to 
outages and strengthening system performance under similar operating stress. 

Transient stability analysis was conducted to ensure that proposed solutions maintained system 
performance during sudden disturbances. This validation step confirmed that upgrades would 
preserve stability and control during dynamic events, further reducing the risk of widespread 
impacts. 

Finally, economic screening highlighted projects that not only addressed reliability concerns 
but also delivered congestion relief and market efficiencies. This ensures that the 
recommended upgrades will provide measurable economic benefits in addition to 
strengthening the grid. 

Taken together, this holistic evaluation ensured that the recommended projects deliver value 
across multiple layers, combining resiliency, operational, and stability benefits with broad 
economic performance. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

127 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

6 PORTFOLIO DEVELOPMENT AND 

PROJECT SELECTION 

6.1  PORTFOLIO DEVELOPMENT PROCESS 

The purpose of the portfolio development process is to develop a consolidated list of projects 
that comprehensively address the system’s needs. Figure 6.1 shows a high-level overview of the 
portfolio development process. The process starts with the utilization of project metrics in 
project grouping and continues through the development of a consolidated portfolio. One 
unique factor regarding this process flow, shown in the figure below for the 2025 ITP solution 
alternatives, that resolve resiliency needs fit into the portfolio development process flowing 
into the “Potential Alternatives” box in the groupings column, and subsequently into the 
appropriate groupings during the optimization and consolidation steps where all projects in 
the respective groupings are deliberated on whether or not they move forward into the final 
portfolio. One unique factor in this process flow, shown in the figure below for the 2025 ITP 
solution alternatives, is how resiliency-driven projects fit into the portfolio development 
process. These projects flow first into the “Potential Alternatives” box in the groupings column, 
then move into the appropriate groupings during the optimization and consolidation steps, 
where all projects are deliberated on before advancing to the final portfolio. 

Figure 6.1: Portfolio Development Process 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

128 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

6.1.1  SOUTHWESTERN PUBLIC SERVICE PORTFOLIO 

DEVELOPMENT PROCESS  

During the needs assessment milestone, SPP staff identified complications in the SPS area 
caused by rapid load growth. In portfolio development, staff created a base-case portfolio to 
stabilize both the reliability and economic models for further analysis. The SPS base-case 
portfolio had two primary goals: improving real power transfer into the SPS area and 
strengthening the underlying 115 kV system where most of the load growth occurred. 

To achieve these goals, staff focused on: 

Identifying a second 765 kV line into the area to ensure increased power transfers 

1. 
2.  Rebuilding lines with base case overloads on the 115 kV system 
3.  Adding multiple 115 kV ties to the existing 345 kV system through bigger or new 

transformers 

4.  Looping several 115 kV radials to further enhance reliability.  

These upgrades eliminated the need for fictitious voltage support in New Mexico and resolved 
most of the emergency energy flagged in the economic models. 

One risk of using a base-case portfolio is that presupposing solutions could overlook the 
benefits of projects submitted through the DPP process. To mitigate this risk, SPP staff 
conducted multiple rounds of project evaluation. In each round, the presupposed 765 kV 
corridor was removed and replaced with an alternative EHV solution submitted during the DPP 
window. This approach ensured a fair comparison between the base-case portfolio and 
stakeholder-submitted projects, allowing staff to select the best corridor into the area. 

6.2  SOLUTION EVALUATION  

SPP evaluated each solution in each applicable model scenario to determine their effectiveness 
in mitigating the needs identified in the needs assessment. 

The solutions evaluated were comprised of: 

•  FERC Order 1000 and Order 890 solutions submitted by stakeholders 
•  SPP staff-developed solutions  
•  Model adjustments and model corrections 

SPP analyzed 2,500 DPPs and approximately 1,000 staff-developed solutions. SPP calculated a 
conceptual cost estimate for each solution based on a standardized conceptual cost template. 
SPP utilized the conceptual cost during solution screening. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

129 

 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

6.2.1  RELIABILITY SOLUTION SCREENING 

SPP tested solutions to determine their ability to mitigate reliability criteria violations in the 
study horizon. SPP deemed solutions to be effective if they resolved system violations to a level 
allowed by the SPP Planning Criteria or members’ more-stringent zonal planning criteria, as 
applicable. Figure 6.2 illustrates the reliability project screening process.  

Reliability metrics were developed by SPP and stakeholders. SPP calculated these metrics for 
each project and used them as a tool to develop a portfolio of projects to address all reliability 
needs. The first metric was a cost per loading relief (CLR) score, which relates the amount of 
thermal loading relief a solution provides to its engineering and construction (E&C) cost. The 
second metric was cost per voltage relief (CVR) score, which relates the amount of voltage 
support a solution provides to its E&C cost. Due to the complexity of the model issues in the 
SPS area, solution evaluation was also performed on models with some key projects pre-
applied to the SPS area in order to accurately evaluate additional reliability project benefits. 

Figure 6.2: Reliability Project Screening Process 

6.2.2 ECONOMIC SOLUTION SCREENING 

SPP evaluated potential solutions to determine their effectiveness in mitigating transmission 
congestion over the 10-year study horizon. For each project, SPP calculated both a one-year 
B/C ratio and a 40-year present value (PV) B/C ratio based on APC savings across all futures and 
study years. 

To determine the one-year benefit to the SPP region for each study year, SPP calculated the 
annual change in APC across all SPP pricing zones. The one-year B/C ratio for each project was 
calculated by dividing this one-year benefit by the one-year cost of the project. The one-year 
cost—also referred to as the projected Annual Transmission Revenue Requirement (ATRR)—

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

130 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

was calculated using the historical SPP median two-year Net Plant Carrying Charge (NPCC) for 
the TO, multiplied by the project’s conceptual cost. For projects assigned to non-SPP TOs, SPP 
applied an SPP-average NPCC of 15.82% in this assessment. The 40-year project cost was 
calculated using these NPCCs, an 8% discount rate, and a 2.0% inflation rate. 

Screening was not viable due to the high levels of emergency energy observed in the Base 
MEM, which continued to mask congestion and resulted in unreliable benefit calculations. 

Initially, staff used an economic model in which preliminary reliability projects were embedded 
to mitigate emergency energy. An event file was employed with selected constraints relaxed to 
values that preserved the identified needs while also unmasking additional congestion by 
mitigating emergency energy. This approach enabled a more accurate ranking and selection of 
projects for portfolio development. 

Subsequently, following the identification of the final reliability project that addressed 
emergency energy, staff incorporated this project into the economic analysis and utilized the 
event file with relaxed constraints for subsequent solution screening. In this approach, reliability 
projects selected through the reliability portfolio development process—specifically those 
addressing emergency energy—were integrated into the economic groupings. This allowed for 
more accurate benefit calculations when conducting subtraction analyses on those groupings. 

6.2.3 PERSISTENT OPERATIONAL SOLUTION SCREENING 

SPP performed persistent operational solution screening by first correlating persistent 
operational needs with reliability or economic needs as applicable. Any persistent operational 
needs correlating to reliability or economic needs were screened using those respective metrics 
to ensure long-term viability of the solution. Solutions for persistent operational needs not 
correlated to reliability or economic needs were identified using the needs information in the 
DPP solution created by SPP staff. These solutions were validated as persistent operational 
solutions using raw state estimator cases and observing changes in thermal loading or voltage 
magnitude for the respective need(s). 

6.2.4 OTHER SOLUTION SCREENINGS  

SPP analyzed the submitted short circuit solutions to ensure that the updated fault-interrupting 
equipment ratings were greater than the maximum fault current identified in the needs 
assessment. Additionally, SPP identified no public policy needs in the 2025 ITP. Therefore, no 
solutions were screened to address public policy needs. 

  TRANSFER ANALYSIS  

A voltage stability assessment was conducted on the year five and year 10 RPM models. The 
transfer boundaries for this study were set to reflect the six LOLE zones. Analysis was performed 
to increase transfer both into and out of each zone. All submitted DPP solutions, and a large 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

131 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

number of staff solutions were evaluated in all 96 transfer scenarios. Solution results were 
compared to the transfer needs (described in the needs assessment section of this report) and 
ranked according to the number of transfer needs that were solved by each project. Stability 
issues in the SPS area required that the BR portfolio for that area be applied to the models and 
a small subset of higher performing transfer projects be re-evaluated. 

6.3  PROJECT SELECTION AND GROUPING  

After screening all solutions, SPP drafted reliability, operational, economic and short circuit 
groupings in parallel to address the different need types across the system. SPP used Study 
Cost Estimates (SCE)23 and stakeholder feedback from direct discussions with stakeholders to 
finalize costs of projects for rankings. Staff utilized-regularly scheduled working group 
meetings, the July 2025 SPP transmission planning summit, SPP’s Request Management System 
(RMS) and the additional ITP Get-Informed Friday meetings to determine project feasibility and 
select final projects.  

6.3.1  STUDY COST ESTIMATES 

SPP evaluated high-performing solutions from the screening assessments conducted during 
the Solution Development and Evaluation milestone to determine if the solutions were 
potentially competitive. SPP sent these solutions to TOs and a third-party estimator for the 
development of study cost within ±30% of the final project cost. Solutions deemed not 
potentially competitive were sent to the incumbent TOs, while potentially competitive solutions 
were submitted to a third-party estimator for the development of SCEs. Once SPP received the 
SCEs, they were utilized throughout the remainder of the portfolio development process. In 
instances where an SCE was not provided by the incumbent TO, SPP used the previously 
calculated conceptual cost estimates (CCE). 

As part of the 2025 ITP cycle, SPP implemented several key enhancements to the cost 
estimating process to improve transparency, accuracy, and alignment with industry best 
practices. One of the most impactful changes was the implementation of RR610, which enabled 
SPP staff to incorporate additional industry data sources and the augmentation of historical 
cost information. This allowed SPP staff to better reflect current market conditions and regional 
construction trends, ultimately leading to more reliable and defensible cost estimates. 

Another major improvement was the early engagement of the PCWG in the review of 
conceptual cost estimates prior to their incorporation into the 2025 ITP study. This proactive 
step ensured that cost assumptions were vetted by stakeholders, reducing the likelihood of 
downstream revisions and increasing confidence in the planning process. Additionally, SPP staff 

23 SPP OATT Business Practices, Section 8 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

132 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

applied lessons learned from the footprint’s first experience estimating 765 kV transmission 
projects in the 2024 ITP. Based on this experience, SPP staff refined estimating methodology 
and coordinated with neighboring regions to align on 765 kV best practices, which helped 
improve assumptions and consistency. One noteworthy example of this refinement was the 
treatment of line mileage assumptions for cost estimation. While the standard ITP practice uses 
120% of straight-line mileage, SPP adopted 130% for new 765 kV lines to better account for 
siting challenges and expected routing adjustments. 

Finally, SPP staff established greater and more consistent coordination with the third-party cost 
estimator throughout the study cycle. This ongoing collaboration ensured that the estimator 
remained aligned with evolving study needs, stakeholder expectations, and regional cost 
trends. Regular touchpoints and feedback helped reinforce consistency in assumptions and 
provided opportunities to clarify scope and methodology. 

6.3.2 RELIABILITY GROUPING 

SPP used a programmatic method to generate a subset of solutions that addressed the 
reliability needs on the system. Solution selection software allowed SPP to systematically 
compare the performance of each solution using the metrics described in section 6.3. During 
this process, SPP applied engineering judgment to develop a draft list of high-performing 
solutions to address reliability needs. 

Some areas required a more in-depth analysis of solutions to address needs. Specifically, the 
unprecedented load growth in North Dakota and southeast New Mexico required a holistic 
approach to develop the reliability grouping. SPP looked ahead to the 2026 ITP load forecast 
which showed continued load growth over the next 10 years in both areas. This suggested that 
SPP needed to plan robust solutions to get ahead of the coming load and prepare for the grid 
of the future. Additional information on the selected projects is given in the Project 
Recommendations section 7. 

SPP continually refined the list of reliability solutions by incorporating stakeholder feedback 
and analysis results. Table 6.1 below shows the final reliability grouping selected to address the 
reliability needs in the 2025 ITP.

6.3.3 ECONOMIC GROUPING  

SPP used an iterative process to develop economic groupings. During the initial project 
screening phase, SPP evaluated each project to determine if it had a one-year B/C ratio of at 
least 0.5 or a 40-year PV B/C ratio of at least 1.0. If a project met either of the criteria, it was 
further evaluated and added to the applicable grouping based on one-year project cost, one-
year APC benefit, 40-year project cost, 40- year PV B/C ratio, and congestion relief for the 
economic needs. Throughout this process, projects were also evaluated against the RMEMs to 
identify solutions that resolved congestion from economic needs that were observed in those 
resiliency models. The initial approach for creating the groupings was to holistically resolve 
congestion in the MEMs and another set of groupings to resolve congestion in the RMEMs. As 
the groupings were developed and projects selected for resolving congestion, staff observed 
that the vast majority of projects selected resolved the congestion observed in both the MEMs 
and RMEMs.  

In line with those results, SPP developed three economic project groupings for each future, 
resulting in six total groupings: 

•  Cost-Effective (CE): Projects with the lowest cost per congestion relief for a single 

economic need 

•  Highest Net APC Benefit (HN): Projects with the highest APC benefit minus project 

cost, with consideration of overlap if multiple projects mitigate congestion on the same 
economic needs 

•  Multi-variable (MV): Projects selected using data from the two other groupings; 

including the flexibility to use additional considerations, such as overlap with other 
portfolios, seams optimization and increasing energy equity across the SPP footprint. 

Table 6.2 identifies a comprehensive list of economic projects included in the four initial 
groupings as well as the multi-variable grouping. Some projects appeared in multiple 
groupings. The multi-variable grouping was developed later based on the results of the initial 
cost-effective and highest-net simulations 

  PROJECT SUBTRACTION EVALUATION 

SPP developed draft groupings using individual project screening results, which tested projects 
by incrementally adding projects to the base MEMs. When assessing a grouping of economic 
solutions, it was necessary to re-evaluate project performance within the grouping to ensure 
the projected APC benefit of each project in the grouping meets the required B/C ratio 
thresholds. SPP used subtraction evaluation to identify when multiple projects were providing 
congestion relief to a constraint. Subtraction analysis also showed which projects are 
dependent on each other to relieve overall system congestion. SPP created new sets of base 
case models per grouping by adding each grouping’s projects, relevant model adjustments and 
model corrections. SPP then removed all economic projects from the models individually to 
determine each project’s APC impact compared to the new base case. SPP removed projects 
that did not meet a 1.0 B/C ratio from the subtraction evaluation from the grouping. SPP 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

136 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

repeated this subtraction evaluation process for each grouping until all remaining projects 
maintained a minimum B/C ratio of 1.0 over 40 years.

6.3.4 SHORT CIRCUIT GROUPING

be rated higher than the maximum fault current identified in the needs assessment. Table 6.4

6.4  OPTIMIZATION 

SPP selected projects for the reliability portfolio based on their ability to be CE, maintain 
reliability, and meet the system’s compliance needs. SPP selected economic projects for the 
economic groupings based on their ability to provide ratepayer benefits from lower-cost 
energy by mitigating system congestion and improving markets for both buyers and sellers. All 
projects were developed using criteria specific to their need and model type. 

SPP evaluated the reliability portfolio to determine its impact on the selected economic 
grouping. Once SPP had developed comprehensive future specific portfolios, SPP assessed the 
impact of the base reliability portfolio and applied economic project subtraction analysis.  

For this cycle, SPP identified three projects that address both reliability needs and economic 
needs: 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

142 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

•  Belfield - Roundup 345 kV New 
•  Columbus Southeast – Columbus 115 kV Line Rebuild 
•  Patentgate – Pioneer 345 kV Line Tap 

The subtraction analysis also identified removal of the Russett North - Russett South 138 kV 
Line Rebuild from the Future 1 economic portfolio. Belfield - Roundup 345 kV New Line was 
selected over the reliability project Belfield - Medora 230 kV Line Rebuild. 

No additional overlap of economic and reliability projects was identified; therefore, all 
remaining reliability, operational, short-circuit, ZPC, and economic projects were included in the 
final optimized portfolio for each future. 

6.5  PORTFOLIO CONSOLIDATION 

To develop a single portfolio for stakeholder recommendation, SPP consolidated the final 
future-specific portfolios. To guide decisions on project inclusion in the consolidated portfolio, 
SPP used a systematic scoring methodology to evaluate project performance. Under this 
approach, three scenarios can occur during the consolidation of the future-specific portfolios 
into a single portfolio: 

1.  The same project is addressing the same or similar needs in both futures. 
2.  Different projects are addressing the same or similar needs in both futures. 
3.  A project addresses certain needs in only one future. 

Projects in scenario 1 are automatically eligible for inclusion in the consolidated portfolio. 
Projects in scenario 2 and 3 require additional assessment to determine eligibility. 

To evaluate projects meeting scenario 2 or 3 conditions, SPP and its stakeholders developed a 
systematic scoring rubric that considers quantitative and qualitative metrics. Quantitative 
metrics included APC B/C ratios and the percentage of congestion relieved. Qualitative metrics 
include crediting projects able to address operational congestion or non-thermal issues. Table 
6.5 details the scoring rubric, and the minimum criteria required for projects to receive points. 
Staff and stakeholders agreed that although this scoring methodology is a good way to 
measure a project’s effectiveness, it should not be the only input to project selection; 
engineering judgment and other considerations may justify choosing a different option. A 
project narrative may accompany recommendations when the preferred project differs from the 
consolidation results.

6.5.3 CONSOLIDATION SCENARIO THREE 

Projects applicable to scenario three must achieve a minimum score of 70 points to be 
considered for consolidation. Scoring parameters are detailed in Table 6.5. For the 2025 ITP, 
seventeen projects were assessed under scenario three. Thirteen were included in the 
consolidated portfolio, with some achieving the minimum score and others selected based on 
engineering judgment.

  765 KV OVERLAY: WOODWARD - VIOLA - ANTHEM - SEMINOLE - MINCO - 
CRAWFISH DRAW 765 KV, SEMINOLE - SOUTHWEST SHREVEPORT 765 KV 
Originated from Future 2. The eastern part of the 765 kV overlay plays a large role in the high 
benefit numbers being observed from the final portfolio. This proposed overlay however does 
not meet the B/C thresholds in future 1 required for automatic inclusion in the final portfolio 
from the consolidation process. When deciding on moving forward with the 765 kV overlay into 
the consolidated portfolio even though the points accumulated do not meet the necessary 
amount, the main driver is comparing the load forecasts from the 2025 ITP futures one and two 
to the most recent forecasts for future ITP study cycles. It becomes clear that the future one 
load assumptions in the 2025 ITP are no longer realistic to the future load amounts being 
observed, and that future two is much more on par with the state of how things will look from 
a load perspective five and 10 years into the future. On that basis, and given the backbone 
value and coordination with previously approved 765 kV facilities, the overlay is included in the 
consolidated portfolio. 

Additional details regarding NTC issuance for the 765kV overlay can be found in section 7.1.

6.6  FINAL CONSOLIDATED PORTFOLIO 

The consolidated portfolio includes the reliability projects addressing steady state and short-
circuit needs, the operational projects, projects that address ZPC needs, and the consolidated 
set of economic projects that met the consolidation criteria. The consolidated portfolio totals 
$19.4 billion and is projected to create $151.5 billion to $248.5 billion in APC savings under 
Future 1 or Future 2 assumptions, respectively. Table 6.23 lists the projects included in the final 
consolidated portfolio along with their classifications and costs. Benefit data reported in this 
section includes only APC savings.

Figure 6.10: Consolidated Portfolio APC Benefits and Costs* 

Figure 6.11 below shows the break-even and payback dates of the consolidated portfolio 
assuming all projects are placed in-service by 1/1/2029. The break-even year is reflective of the 
first year that the one-year APC benefits are expected to outweigh the portfolio ATRR. The 
payback year is reflective of the year that the cumulative APC benefits are expected to exceed 
the 40-year PV costs of the portfolio. The consolidated portfolio is expected to break even 
within the first year of being placed in service and expected to pay back total investment within 
the first two years. This calculation provides a measure of comfort that SPP’s members will see 
a quick return on investment in the recommended portfolio. Realistically, this payback period 
will not occur because not all projects in the consolidated portfolio will receive an NTC, nor will 
they be in-service by 2029. 

* Following study completion, staff received minimal updates to project cost estimates. The benefit-cost 
calculations presented here are based on the final study portfolio and do not incorporate those 
subsequent updates.

6.7  STAGING 

Staging is the process by which the need date for each project is determined. The staging 
methodology can be found in the ITP Manual section 6.3. The staging methodology for 
resiliency projects can be found in the 2025 ITP Scope. 

The following gives an overview of the methodology used for each project type: 

•  Reliability projects were staged based on the year and season when the needs 

addressed by each project appear on the transmission system.  

•  Economic projects were staged based on the first year that the B/C ratio exceeds 1.0.  
•  Operational projects were staged with a need date of 11/18/2025. This is the date of 

NTC issuance following approval by the Board.  

•  Age and Condition projects and projects that address ZPC needs were staged using the 

in-service date provided by the TO. 

SPP adjusted the project lead times from 48 months to 60 months for EHV projects spanning 
more than 100 miles. This adjustment gives more realistic in-service dates and improves future 
cycle model quality. Additionally, if any utilities provided feedback on lead times for certain 
projects, staff utilized the TO recommended lead time.  

* Following study completion, staff received minimal updates to project cost estimates. The benefit-cost 
calculations presented here are based on the final study portfolio and do not incorporate those 
subsequent updates. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

166 

 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

SPP staff identified several study complexities that made staging decisions challenging. These 
included highly stressed models, updates from stakeholders, target area analyses, resiliency 
conditions, and the 765 kV overlay. The staging results are based solely on the 2025 ITP 
models; the 2026 ITP models with updated load forecasts were not used. 

The SPS area required special attention. By summer of year two, at least one 765 kV line is 
necessary to support voltage and serve the southern New Mexico load. This could be either the 
Potter–Crossroads–Phantom 765 kV line from the 2024 ITP or the Crawfish Draw–Phantom 765 
kV line. Without one of these lines, the models indicate voltage collapse. 

Updated stakeholder information delayed the in-service date for the Potter–Crossroads–
Phantom line to 2031. With this change, the Crawfish Draw–Phantom line became critical by 
summer 2026. By summer of year five, both the Potter–Crossroads–Phantom and Crawfish 
Draw–Phantom lines are needed. A second Crawfish Draw–Phantom 765 kV circuit is required 
by year five to prevent N-1 voltage collapse. By year 10, the Woodward–Crawfish Draw 765 kV 
line is needed to supply additional MWs to southern New Mexico. 

Many lower-voltage projects in the SPS area support large power transfers from the 765 kV 
system to local loads. As a result, the need dates for many of these lower-voltage projects align 
with the 765 kV line need dates. 

The staging dates for the consolidated portfolio of projects are shown below in Table 6.25, 
results of the staging milestone are used to determine NTC recommendations. The final NTC 
recommendations are shown in Table 9.1 at the end of this report.

7 PROJECT RECOMMENDATIONS 

7.1  765 KV TRANSMISSION OVERLAY – PHASE 1 

As noted in the study driver section, the extreme load growth observed in the 2025 and 2026 
ITP assessments necessitates a significant transmission investment. Because the load growth has 
shifted from concentrated areas (2024 ITP) to a more wide-ranging area, and the loads added 
are large in magnitude, staff and stakeholders alike considered how to best serve this increased 
load: 

•  Continued build out of the 345 kV system 
Increased use of 500 kV transmission 
• 
•  Continuation of 765 kV expansion from the 765 kV line from Phantom - Crossroads – 

Potter County issued in the 2024 ITP 

As SPP staff considered these alternatives, it became apparent that the transfer and voltage 
support needs justified 765 kV solutions.  

SPP previously evaluated 765 and 500 kV overlay infrastructure in 2007. At the time load growth 
was limited to 1-2% growth. Additionally, SPP was evaluating the economic potential of a 
growing wind fleet. Even with the extremely low cost to build 765 kV infrastructure compared to 
today, the benefit to cost ratio of the 765/500 kV backbone was identified to be around 1.0, 
which was not enough to justify the investment. SPP’s EHV infrastructure at the time was limited. 
Supporting a 500 or 765 kV backbone with limited underlying EHV would have been extremely 
difficult to manage operationally. Ultimately, SPP elected to move forward with building out the 
345 kV network further. 

Since that decision was made, SPP has invested over $10 billion dollars to improve the 345 kV 
network to equip SPP with the necessary infrastructure to support a 765 kV backbone. 

As projected, load growth was limited over the next 15 years between 1-2% annually and the 
connection of new wind generation continued; however, where SPP stands today is far from the 
expected future of 2007. The last two years have seen load growth explode, and SPP has far 
outpaced the expected connection of wind generation to its system. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

172 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

Figure 7.1: 2007 SPP EHV Overlay Study Proposed Paths 

Figure 7.1 shows some of the 765 kV and 500 kV paths that were evaluated in 2007. The 
parallels between the 2007 plan and the recommended 765 kV overlay in the 2025 ITP are 
evident. Viola, Seminole, Tuco27, Woodward, and Potter appear prominently in both plans.  

The figure below represents the first phase of a 765 kV backbone for the SPP footprint. The 
overlay can be broken down into three pieces:  

1.  Reliability – Creating a loop of 765 kV infrastructure to address load growth in 

the New Mexico area 

2.  Economic/Resiliency – Connecting areas of conventional and renewable 
generation to identified areas of load growth (New Mexico and Tulsa) 
3.  Target Area – a holistic solution to address the 2025 ITP Target Area 

Each piece of the overlay, and the accompanying benefits, are explained in the following 
sections. As noted in the section title, this overlay represents a Phase 1 of bringing SPP a much 
needed 765 kV backbone. Later ITP studies such as the 2026 ITP and CPP Transition Assessment 
will evaluate the need for further 765 kV moving north to other areas of the SPP footprint where 
spot loads were submitted for evaluation for the first time.  

27 Tuco is an existing 345 kV bus in the SPP today, Early versions of the 765 kV overlay in the 2025 ITP 
identified Tuco as a key bus, however, there is no nearby land to build 765 kV infrastructure. Crawfish 
Draw is located close by and will connect to the 345 kV system at Tuco through a 345 kV line from 
Crawfish Draw to Tuco. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

173 

 
 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

Figure 7.2: 765 kV Overlay 

7.1.1  RELIABILITY: POTTER COUNTY – WOODWARD – CRAWFISH 

DRAW 765 KV & CRAWFISH DRAW – PHANTOM DOUBLE-
CIRCUIT 765 KV 

The reliability component of the proposed 765 kV overlay builds upon the initial Potter–
Crossroads–Phantom 765 kV line included in the 2024 ITP assessment. At that time, staff 
identified the line as the most effective next step to mitigate voltage limitations and 
transmission interface loading issues in New Mexico driven by significant load growth. 

Subsequent analysis using finalized 2025 ITP data demonstrated that a single 765 kV facility 
would not provide adequate energy delivery capability or voltage support for the region. 
Furthermore, the Potter–Crossroads–Phantom line itself emerged as the new most impactful 
contingency. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

174 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

Contingency analysis within SPP’s 2025 ITP BR models produced over 57,000 non-converged 
contingencies—scenarios in which the power flow model failed to reach a valid solution. To 
obtain results, staff performed manual intervention by applying alternative solution parameters. 
A large portion of these non-converged cases indicated voltage collapse, which would represent 
a severe and potentially widespread service disruption under real-world operating conditions. 

Figure 7.3 illustrates the substantial load forecast growth within the SPS area, which increased 
nearly **2.5 times—from approximately 4,700 MW to 11,500 MW—**between the 2023 and 
2025 ITP forecast cycles. Load growth of this magnitude would necessitate major transmission 
upgrades in any localized area. Given the SPS area’s physical and electrical isolation from the 
broader SPP network, establishing highly efficient bulk power delivery capability is critical, which 
is why 765 kV is the right solution. 

SPP’s economic planning simulations further reflected this constraint, with emergency energy28 
conditions occurring during nearly all modeled hours, clearly signaling that the existing 
transmission infrastructure could not reliably serve the projected demand.

Figure 7.3: SPS Load Growth 

To address these reliability deficiencies, SPP identified the need for a new 765 kV transmission 
facility originating at Crawfish Draw (north of Amarillo, Texas), where robust connections to the 

28 Emergency energy in an adjusted production cost model occurs when there is no remaining redispatch 
option available to serve load without overloading a transmission constraint. Emergency energy is 
assigned an extremely high cost to prevent it from occurring. In effect, high amounts of emergency 
energy signify a likely reliability issue.  

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

175 

 
 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

existing 345 kV system are available. This new 765 kV line to Phantom was incorporated to 
address early-year needs within the 2025 ITP study horizon. 

By year 10 of the study horizon, analysis indicated a second circuit from Crawfish Draw was 
needed along with the requirement for an additional 765 kV path from Crawfish Draw to an 
alternative termination point with strong generation access. Woodward was selected as the 
optimal termination point due to its proximity to large quantities of low-cost wind generation 
and its established 345 kV network strength. This configuration enables delivery of renewable 
resources to meet New Mexico’s growing demand while maintaining grid stability. 

Finally, SPP incorporated the Potter–Woodward 765 kV line to complete an EHV transmission 
loop. While this facility does not directly address voltage or MW delivery concerns within the BR 
models, it establishes looped redundancy within the 765 kV overlay. This topology ensures that, 
if any individual segment of the 765 kV system is out of service, energy can still flow through 
alternate paths. Economic analysis demonstrates that this added network resilience provides a 
strong long-term investment value. 

NTC recommendations are limited to the Woodward–Crawfish Draw–Phantom segments of this 
reliability portfolio. The second circuit from Crawfish Draw–Phantom is not recommended for 
NTC issuance at this time. 

The Potter–Woodward project is particularly significant because it closes the 765 kV loop serving 
Texas and New Mexico load growth. Completing this loop is essential to unlock the full reliability 
and economic benefits of the 765 kV overlay in the southern portion of the system. However, by 
deferring the NTC recommendation for Potter–Woodward at this stage, SPP preserves the 
flexibility to evaluate whether more strategic or cost-effective alternatives could achieve the 
same looping function. In the 2026 ITP and beyond, staff will continue to optimize the overlay to 
ensure the loop is closed in a way that maximizes regional value while balancing cost, 
constructability, and long-term system needs. 

The decision to remove the second Crawfish Draw–Phantom circuit was driven primarily by 
constructability concerns. While a third circuit into Phantom remains critical for long-term 
reliability, building a double-circuit 765 kV line would be exceptionally difficult—global 
precedent suggests only one such facility currently exists. In future planning studies, SPP will 
evaluate alternative paths to deliver this third circuit into the Phantom area, ensuring reliability 
needs are met with more practical and feasible solutions. 

7.1.2  ECONOMIC/RESILIENCY: WOODWARD – VIOLA – ANTHEM – 

SEMINOLE – MINCO – CRAWFISH DRAW 765 KV 

The economic and resiliency component of the 2025 ITP 765 kV overlay focuses on connecting 
strategic grid locations where load is expected to grow east of Tulsa to key system buses 
electrically close to dispatchable and/or low-cost renewable energy. Each selected bus also 
requires a strong, established 345 kV connection with multiple lines terminating there. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

176 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

The large load resiliency condition imposed substantial stress on the system east of Tulsa, near 
the new Anthem 345 kV bus. As the study progressed, stakeholder discussions and 2026 ITP 
data confirmed both the certainty of future load growth and its magnitude would rise further. 
This level of load required development of a new 765 kV line to serve the area. 

Analysis of potential solutions connecting renewable generation in central and western Kansas 
identified high-value opportunities. Extending from Woodward, where the 765 kV line already 
supports New Mexico loads, to Anthem via Viola emerged as a particularly beneficial project. 
Viola lies near the SPP Y-Plan, a set of double-circuit 345 kV lines designed to export wind from 
western Kansas to the east. By strategically linking these key buses, SPP avoids relying on the 
existing 345 kV system, which already experiences heavy congestion from west-to-east wind 
flows. 

Similar to the looped 765 kV system serving New Mexico, losing the 765 kV line into Anthem 
would create significant stress on the network. As a result, SPP identified the need for a second 
765 kV line into Anthem. The Seminole bus, southeast of Oklahoma City, presented an optimal 
connection point due to its strong 345 kV network and access to both existing and planned 
generation. 

Continuing the 765 kV westward through Minco, another bus with renewable connections, and 
ultimately to Crawfish Draw, SPP completed the Economic/Resiliency loop, creating a robust 
path for energy delivery and enhancing grid flexibility. 

Similar to the projects discussed in section7.1.1, certain portions of the economic and resiliency 
elements of the 765 kV overlay are not recommended for NTC issuance at this time. Specifically, 
the Woodward–Viola and Viola–Anthem 765 kV lines have been removed from the current 
recommendation. 

By deferring NTC issuance on these lines, SPP staff can continue to refine and optimize the 
northern portion of the 765 kV overlay in the 2026 ITP, ensuring that customers receive the 
greatest value while SPP develops the most effective transmission solutions. Given the pace and 
scale of load growth—especially as demand expands north into Kansas and beyond—further 
transmission investment in this area is expected. 

It is important to underscore, however, that creating a complete 765 kV loop remains essential. 
Although these particular line segments are not recommended at this stage, the loop will 
ultimately need to be closed either through their inclusion or through alternative 765 kV 
facilities identified in future studies. Without completing the loop, SPP risks losing a substantial 
portion of the potential benefits—such as enhanced reliability, improved resilience to extreme 
events, and long-term economic savings—that go well beyond the immediate cost justification 
of any single project. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

177 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

7.1.3  TARGET AREA: SEMINOLE – SOUTHWEST SHREVEPORT 765 

KV 

The Target Area is experiencing instability, low voltages, and congestion on the 
PSOSWEPCOTIES interface, along with inlet and outlet limitations that stress the existing 
transmission system. Rapid load growth in the area has further increased system stress, and 
future load and generation additions are expected to continue this trend. Strengthening the 
transmission network is critical to maintain reliability, improve operational flexibility, and 
alleviate economic congestion across the interface. 

To address these needs, SPP staff recommends constructing a 765 kV line from Seminole to 
Southwest Shreveport, paired with two 300 Megavar (MVAR) Static Synchronous Compensators 
(STATCOMs)—one at Diana and one at Southwest Shreveport. This high-capacity line will 
provide a robust backbone into the Target Area, reinforcing the existing infrastructure while 
enabling future load and generation integration. In addition to resolving voltage and stability 
concerns, the line reduces reliance on the constrained 345 kV network, creates a stronger long-
distance transfer path, and enhances resiliency against future load growth. 

SPP staff also evaluated multiple 345 kV alternatives. The 345 kV solution initially appeared in 
the Future 1 economic groupings but was removed because its B/C values did not meet the 
threshold for the final portfolio. The 765 kV option outperformed it in multiple ways: 

•  Stability performance: The 765 kV solution produced more stable conditions under 

simulated P3, P4, P5, and P6 events. 

•  Economic impact: It most effectively reduced separation between SWEPCO and the rest 

of SPP and delivered the highest economic benefit. 

•  Right-sized for future growth: With one GW of spot load projected in the 2025 ITP, 500 
MW submitted as firm in the 2026 ITP, and another 1,000 MW with high confidence, the 
765 kV line can accommodate long-term growth in the Shreveport area. 

In summary, the 765 kV alternative addresses the critical system needs, provides superior 
stability and economic benefits, and delivers the capacity required to support long-term 
reliability and expansion in the Target Area. 

7.2  RESILIENCY BENEFITS OF THE PORTFOLIO 

Resiliency needs were greatly improved by the 765 kV overlay. Winter Storm Uri pushed 
transmission assets to the brink with congestion, especially with north-to-south and west-to-
east powerflows—the latter the overlay specifically addresses.  

The 765 kV overlay releases congestion and provides significant new capacity that both reduces 
generation curtailment and improve transferability between sub-regions. In fact, the full 
portfolio with the 765 kV overlay solves 98.7% of all resiliency transfer needs that were identified 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

178 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

in the needs assessment. The overlay also significantly reduces the dispatch of fictitious 
emergency energy in the MEM. And lastly, with the portfolio, load shed was reduced by 52 to 
72% with remaining contingency voltages reduced by up to 93% in the SCRD analysis and 100% 
in the cascading analysis.

Figure 7.5: Economic Portfolio APC Benefits and Costs under RMEM (2025$)* 

As shown in Figure 7.5, the Economic Portfolio has high APC benefits compared to its cost, with 
particularly strong performance in Future 2 where congestion and production cost savings are 
larger due to the higher loads. Results are consistent with the economic projects targeting the 
dominant congestion paths that are relieved by the overlay. 

The figures above assume resiliency conditions occur every year. That assumption is 
hypothetical and unlikely. Results below include sensitivity blends that assume resiliency 
conditions occur periodically rather than annually. Using the 40-year analysis, SPP formed two 
sensitivity series. For the three-year blended values, years one and two use MEM benefits and 
every 3rd year is replaced with RMEM benefits, repeating the pattern across the 40-year cost 
calculation horizon. Similarly, for the five-year blended values, every 5th year is replaced with 
RMEM benefits. Applying the same escalation and discounting used in the 40-year workbook to 
convert annual APC benefits to real 2025 dollars and compute portfolio B/C with study cost held 
constant for comparability. Figure 7.6 presents the three-year blended resiliency view for the 
final portfolio. Blending across multiple study years smooths weather- and load-driven variability 
while preserving the congestion relief provided by the overlay.
