7.3  RELIABILITY, ECONOMIC, AND PERSISTENT 

OPERATIONAL PROJECTS 

The proposed transmission investments directly support regional planning goals and address a 
high volume of system violations, offering a focused, high-impact response to critical reliability 
challenges. Designed with long-term value in mind, these projects provide durable operational 
and economic solutions while supporting regional growth and adapting to evolving energy 
needs. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

182 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

7.3.1  AMERICAN ELECTRIC POWER (AEP) 

  ANTHEM – GUAVA 345/138 KV TRANSFORMER (RELIABILITY) 

Figure 7.8: AEP: Anthem 345/138 kV Transformer 

East of Tulsa, near Sportsman Acres, the 115 kV system overloads with the loss of either 138 kV 
line from Guava - Inola Tap or Catoosa – Inola Tap. The power normally supplied by Catoosa, 
and the surrounding 345 kV system, must then flow in excess across the 115 kV lines from Kerr 
Grove - Pryor Junction to support the 138 kV system between Tulsa and Sportsman Acres. 
Installing a 345/138 kV transformer between Anthem - Guava provides an additional source of 
support, enabling either Anthem or Catoosa to serve the 138 kV system without overloading any 
of the 115 kV network. This transformer addition creates two robust connections from the 345 
kV backbone to the area east of Tulsa, improving reliability and reducing the risk of 115 kV 
overloads under contingency conditions.  

With the addition of the 765 kV line at Anthem, there is increased need for an outlet to transfer 
power from the EHV system into the lower-voltage network. Installing the 345/138 kV 
transformer at Anthem - Guava provides that outlet, allowing power from the 765 kV system to 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

183 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

flow directly into the 138 kV system. This strengthens transfer capability, enhances the 
effectiveness of the new 765 kV line, and further improves reliability in the east Tulsa area. 

7.3.2 EVERGY-GREATER MISSOURI OPERATIONS (GMO) 

  MARYVILLE – MIDWAY – AVENUE CITY – ST. JOSEPH 161 KV LINE REBUILDS 

(PERSISTENT OPERATIONAL) 

Figure 7.9: GMO: Maryville – Midway – Avenue City – St. Joseph 161 kV Line Rebuilds 

The 161 kV line from Maryville – Midway, located between Kansas City and Lincoln, has been 
identified as an operational need due to 14 cumulative days of thermal exceedances. This facility 
is consistently overloaded during normal operation, even without contingency conditions, 
placing strain on equipment and reducing long-term reliability. Rebuilding the 43-mile 161 kV 
corridor between Maryville and St. Joseph will increase thermal capacity, eliminate recurring 
overloads, support anticipated future growth in the region, and reduce the risk of similar needs 
emerging along this critical corridor. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

184 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

  161 KV NORTH CONGRESS AREA REBUILDS (ECONOMIC/PERSISTENT 

OPERATIONAL) 

Figure 7.10: GMO: 161 kV North Congress Area Rebuilds 

In northwestern Missouri, the 161 kV lines from North Congress - TWA and First Creek - 
Roanridge experience congestion for the loss of the 161 kV line from KCI - Platte City in both 
the MEM and RMEMs. Additionally, First Creek – Roanridge 161 kV experiences real-time 
congestion with the loss of Hawthorne - Nashua 345 kV. The most cost-effective project to 
address these needs is to rebuild both monitored elements, but upon doing so, congestion is 
observed on the rest of the 161 kV corridor on either side of these elements. Since the ratings 
for the lines within this 161 kV corridor are nearly half of the standard rating for 161 kV lines, the 
recommendation is to rebuild the entire corridor to relieve the economic and operational 
congestion. This adequately relieves the congestion and provides significant economic benefit. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

185 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

7.3.3 EVERGY-KANSAS CENTRAL, INC. (EKC) 

  SMOKEY HILLS – SUMMIT 230 KV LINE REBUILD (PERSISTENT OPERATIONAL) 

Figure 7.11: EKC: Smokey Hills – Summit 230 kV Line Rebuild 

The 230 kV line from Smokey Hills – Summit in central Kansas experiences congestion for the 
loss of the 345 kV line from Axtell - Macon. This line has been an operational challenge since 
September of 2023 and has been experiencing congestion for about 16% of the time since then. 
The most economical route to alleviate the congestion is to rebuild the 230 kV line from Smokey 
Hills - Summit. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

186 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

  KNOLL – NORTH HAYS 115 KV LINE REBUILD AND NORTH HAYS – CHETOLAH 

CREEK 115 KV NEW LINE (PERSISTENT OPERATIONAL) 

Figure 7.12: EKC: Knoll – North Hays 115 kV Line Rebuild and North Hays – Chetolah Creek 115 kV New Line 

The 115 kV line from Knoll - North Hays overloads in real time for the loss of the 230 kV line 
from Postrock - South Hays in northwestern Kansas. This line experienced outages for 730 days 
ranging from 2021 - 2023. The most feasible project to address these needs is to rebuild the 115 
kV line from Knoll - North Hays and build a new 115 kV line from Chetolah Creek – North Hays. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

187 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

  JARBALO JCT. – MUND 115 KV TERMINAL EQUIPMENT UPGRADES (ECONOMIC) 

Figure 7.13: EKC: Pentagon Area 115 kV Terminal Equipment Upgrades 

Southwest of Kansas City the 115 kV line from Mund - Pentagon experiences congestion for the 
loss of the 345 kV line from Craig – 87th Street in both the MEM and RMEMs. The monitored 
element is limited by the terminal equipment so upgrading the terminal equipment relieves the 
congestion on this monitored element, but also moves it downstream. Upgrading the terminal 
equipment along the remainder of the corridor allows congestion to dissipate into a part of the 
system that can accommodate the additional flow. The conductor along this corridor is higher 
performing than the standard for a 115 kV line; therefore, once the terminal equipment 
upgrades are implemented, the lines can achieve ratings of 452/510 MegaVolt-Ampere (MVA), 
which adequately relieves the observed congestion. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

188 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

7.3.4 EVERGY METRO (EM) 

  CROSSTOWN – BLUE VALLEY STATION 161 KV NEW LINE (RELIABILITY) 

Figure 7.14: EM: Crosstown – Blue Valley Station 161 kV New Line  

In the heart of Kansas City, the 161 kV line from Crosstown – Grand Avenue West overloads for 
several contingencies, including P3 events, the worst of which is the loss of Charlotte - 
Northeast. Also, for the same contingency, economic congestion is observed in both the MEM 
and RMEMs. The projects considered were a second circuit 161 kV line from Crosstown – Grand 
Avenue West substation, a new 161 kV line from Crosstown – Grand Avenue West, or a new 161 
kV line from Blue Valley Station - Crosstown. Upon further investigation, it was determined that 
neither the Grand Avenue West nor Charlotte substations can be expanded. Considering that 
information, the clear project choice to address these needs is a new 161 kV line from Blue 
Valley Station - Crosstown. Although this project does address the economic congestion, the 
B/C ratio dipped below the necessary threshold for inclusion. The project was removed from the 
consolidated economic grouping but can still be found in the final recommended portfolio. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

189 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

  CRAIG – LENEXA SOUTH 161 KV LINE REBUILD (PERSISTENT OPERATIONAL) 

Figure 7.15: EM: Craig – Lenexa South 161 kV Line Rebuild  

The 161 kV circuit two line from Craig - Lenexa South congests in real time for the loss of the 
161 kV circuit one line. Congestion costs have reached $41,479,257 which exceeds the $10 
million operational need threshold by more than four times. A CE solution is to rebuild the 161 
kV circuit two line from Craig - Lenexa South which relieves the congestion on this line. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

190 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

  CLINTON – STILWELL 345 KV DOUBLE-CIRCUIT NEW LINE (ECONOMIC) 

Figure 7.16: EM: Clinton – Stilwell 345 kV Double-Circuit New Line 

Along the Kansas and Missouri border, the 161 kV line from Clinton - Stilwell is experiencing 
congestion in year five and year 10 in both the MEM and RMEMs for the loss of the 345 kV line 
from Overton - Sibley. In previous cycles a project to create a new 345 kV path across the SPP-
MISO seam was investigated. This new path begins on the eastern half of Missouri at the 
Overton and Loose Creek substations, with new 345 kV lines from each bus converging at the 
Barnett substation. From Barnett, the 345 kV line expands to a double circuit and continues west 
through Clinton and then into the SPP footprint to an appropriate substation—early versions of 
the project identified La Cygne as the endpoint. The new 345 kV path from this analysis was in 
consideration in the 2025 ITP as an alternative to resolve the congestion. It is important to note 
that the costs evaluated for this solution only included the double circuit line from Clinton to the 
termination point on the SPP system and the costs to upgrade the termination point on SPP’s 
system to accommodate a new double circuit line. No substation or expansion costs at the 
Clinton station were included in the B/C evaluation of this solution.  

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

191 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

Alternatives that were also considered include solutions from rebuilding the existing Clinton - 
Stilwell 161 kV line to bolstering the 345 kV path from Overton - Sibley and further west across 
the state of Kansas. The rebuild option alone did not provide enough congestion relief to 
resolve the economic need, and the 345 kV path from Overton to and across Kansas was not a 
cost-effective alternative justifiable through economic analysis. 

When evaluating alternatives to resolve this congestion, multiple points within SPP were 
considered for the most western termination point of this project, those points being the La 
Cygne, Stilwell, Peculiar, and Pleasant Hill substations. Through economic analysis and strategic 
decisions, the Stilwell substation was determined to be the most beneficial western termination 
point for this project, and significantly reduces the congestion observed across the existing 
Clinton - Stilwell 161 kV line.  

As shown in the economic analysis above, the benefit-to-cost ratio of this project did not meet 
the 1.0 B/C ratio requirement. At this time, SPP is not recommending issuance of an NTC for this 
facility through the 2025 ITP. SPP recommends moving this project to a joint study to further 
evaluate this project and a cost split arrangement with the applicable third parties. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

192 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc.

the congestion and overload of this line, SPP recommends rebuilding the Columbus – Columbus

  HOSKINS 345/230 KV NEW TRANSFORMER (ECONOMIC) 

Figure 7.20: NPPD: Hoskins 345/230 kV New Transformer  

In Norfolk, Nebraska, the 345/230 kV transformer at the Hoskins substation experiences 
economic congestion in the MEM and RMEM  in years five and 10 when either the 345 kV line 
from Hoskins - Raun or the 345/115 kV transformer at Hoskins is out of service. This upgrade 
was originally identified and assigned through an Interconnection Agreement out of the DISIS 
study process, but after several generators withdrew their requests, it is no longer needed based 
on subsequent restudies. While no longer needed for those processes, this project is still 
identified as necessary in the ITP. SPP considered multiple projects to address this congestion 
including replacing the existing transformer with a larger capacity transformer or adding a 
second transformer. Ultimately, SPP recommends replacing the existing transformer with a 
larger capacity transformer, increasing the post contingency capacity from 336 MVA to 558 
MVA. This increase in capacity is sufficient to address 100% of the observed congestion.

7.3.7 OKLAHOMA GAS AND ELECTRIC (OGE) 

  OKLAHOMA CITY PROJECTS (RELIABILITY) 

Figure 7.21: OGE: Oklahoma City Projects 

East of Oklahoma City, near Horseshoe Lake, is a large generation facility. The increased 
generation expected at this facility is flowing directly to Oklahoma City to support the load 
growth in the city. In the base case scenario for the year five and year 10 models SPP observed 
thermal overloads across multiple 69 kV and 138 kV paths from Horseshoe Lake – Oklahoma 
City. SPP recommends converting the 69 kV path from Horseshoe Lake - Reno to 138 kV and 
rebuilding the 138 kV paths from Memorial – Trosper, spanning vertically across Oklahoma City. 
The northern path only requires terminal upgrades for the 138 kV lines up through Bryant. The 
increased capacity in these upgraded 138 kV facilities alleviates the overloads and allows for 
more efficient power delivery across Oklahoma City amidst increasing load and generation. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

197 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

  MUSKOGEE – KEEFTON – PECAN CREEK 345 KV NEW LINE (RELIABILITY) 

Figure 7.22: OGE: Muskogee – Keefton – Pecan Creek 345 kV New Line 

In Eastern Oklahoma, south of Muskogee, Oklahoma, there is an existing disconnected 345 kV 
line from Pecan Creek to the Fort Smith – Muskogee 345 kV line. There are multiple large loads 
near Muskogee causing thermal overloads on the Griffin - Muskogee 345 kV and Muskogee – 
Muskogee Tap 161 kV lines in the year 10 summer model.  

SPP staff recommends tapping the 345 kV lines from Muskogee – Fort Smith and from 
Muskogee – Oktaha at the new Keefton switching station, then re-energizing 10 miles of the 
existing 345 kV line that originally connected Pecan Creek to Fort Smtih, using it to connect 
Keefton switching station to Pecan Creek, which will require extending it an additional two miles 
to the Pecan Creek substation. Doing so will alleviate the overload and gives additional 
headroom for the increase in large loads submitted for the 2026 ITP.

strengthening reliability for Oklahoma City load under contingency conditions.

7.3.8 SOUTHWESTERN POWER ADMINISTRATION (SWPA) 

  DIAMOND TAP - CARTHAGE 69 KV LINE REBUILD (RELIABILITY) 

Figure 7.24: SWPA: Diamond Tap - Carthage 69 kV Line Rebuild 

In southwestern Missouri, on the outskirts of Joplin, the 69 kV line from Carthage – Diamond 
Tap experiences a thermal overload for the loss of the 69 kV line from Miami – Miami Tap, 
located in the northeast corner of Oklahoma. SPP observed this violation in the year two, five 
and 10 summer models with the year 10 thermal overload escalating to 110%. The existing 
equipment has a relatively low rating of 35 MVA, as a result, SPP recommends rebuilding the 69 
kV line from Carthage – Diamond Tap to increase the capacity to 70 MVA. Increasing the line’s 
capacity reduces the overload to approximately 53%, enhancing contingency performance and 
supporting more efficient power delivery to the local area. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report

Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

  DARDANELLE DAM - CLARKSVILLE 161 KV TERMINAL UPGRADE 

(RELIABILITY/ECONOMIC) 

Figure 7.25: SWPA: Dardanelle Dam - Clarksville 161 kV Terminal Upgrade 

Along the Arkansas river in western Arkansas, the 161 kV line from Clarksville - Dardanelle 
experiences a thermal overload for the loss of various 500 kV lines connecting to the Arkansas 
Nuclear One substation. The most severe loading of 107% occurs for the loss of two 500 kV lines 
connecting the Arkansas Nuclear One power plant substation to the Mabelvale and Forth Smith 
substations. The MEMs show congestion on the Dardanelle - Clarksville 161 kV line for the loss 
of the 500 kV line from the Arkansas Nuclear One substation to Fort Smith in year 10. The 
RMEMs show congestion for the same constraint in years five and 10. 

SPP staff considered the benefits of rebuilding the 161 kV line from Dardanelle - Clarksville or 
upgrading the terminal equipment of the line. By upgrading the terminal equipment of this line, 
the post contingency rating can be increased from 201/223 (SE/WE) MVA to 329/357 (SE/WE) 
MVA. SPP recommends upgrading the terminal equipment as it resolves the observed thermal 
violations and economic congestion at a lower cost than a full rebuild. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report

Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

7.3.9 SOUTHWESTERN PUBLIC SERVICE (SPS) 

  SPS PROJECTS (RELIABILITY/ECONOMIC) 

Figure 7.26: SPS Projects 

As detailed in section 6, the reliability projects selected for this area were meant to address the 
base-case thermal and voltage issues in the area. These needs were driven by the significant 
load growth in the area along the 115 kV system. The load increased almost three GW between 
the 2024 and 2025 study cycles. This load growth mostly represents increased power usage tied 
to electrification of drilling load along the Texas/New Mexico border. Insufficient transmission 
inlet and outlet facilities prohibiting adequate power transfer needs drove SPP staff to select a 
large-scale transmission corridor into the area, as well as multiple upgrades and tie-ins to the 
115 kV system to support this localized load. Several routes into the area were considered, 
including 345, 500, and 765 kV solutions, as well as HV DC lines (HVDC). Ultimately, the Potter 
Co – Woodward – Crawfish Draw – Phantom 765 kV line was chosen. This line ties to the 
previously-approved 765 kV line at Potter Co from the 2024 ITP, enhancing transmission into the 
area by providing an alternate route. This line was also recognized during the investigation into 
765 kV opportunities that SPP conducted in 2007.  

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

202 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

To support the 115 kV facilities specifically, SPP selected five different ties to the 115 kV system 
from the 345 kV system, as well as a number of rebuilds and double circuits on the 115 kV. The 
large radial loads were also tied in to reduce voltage drag on the surrounding system.  

Lastly, to support the increased demands on the Potter substation, SPP selected both rebuilds of 
existing 230 kV lines as well as new 230 kV lines feeding into Potter. These upgrades also 
include a new 345/230 kV transformer at Potter.

7.3.10 UPPER MISSOURI ZONE (UMZ) 

  EXIRA – ANITA – CRESTON 161 KV RECONFIGURATION 

Figure 7.27: UMZ: Exira – Anita – Creston 161 kV Reconfiguration 

In southwest Iowa, the 161 kV line from Anita Tap – Anita experiences congestion, for the loss of 
the 115 kV line from Macksburg – Winterset Junction, in the Future 1 and Future 2 year 10 MEM 
models. SPP staff collaborated with the local TOs to determine the best solutions to address the 
congestion and understand logistical challenges. SPP recommends utilizing the existing lines 
from Exira - Anita Tap, Anita Tap - Anita and Anita Tap - Creston. To complement this, the Anita 
Tap 161 kV substation will be removed and the lines will directly connect Exira - Anita and Anita 
– Creston while following the existing right-of-way. This will require constructing a 4.5-mile 
extension of new 161 kV line, extending the line from Exira – Anita Tap on to Anita. The portion 
running along the existing path from Anita Tap - Anita will be run as a double circuit. This 
project resolves all congestion observed. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

205 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

  LINDAHL - SIMPSON 115 KV (RELIABILITY) 

Figure 7.28: UMZ: Lindahl - Simpson 115 kV 

The Lindahl – Simpson 115 kV line, just northwest of Tioga, experiences a thermal overload with 

the loss of Liberty – Lindahl 115 kV. These exceedances are observed in the year five and year 10 

light load cases. A rebuild of the line was initially considered, but further analysis showed that 

the jumpers at Simpson were limiting the facility’s rating. Upgrading these jumpers nearly 

doubles the rating of Lindahl – Simpson, mitigating the overload and allowing the line to carry 

the additional power under contingency conditions. By nearly doubling the rating of the Lindahl 

– Simpson line, these jumper upgrades reduce the overload from slightly above 100% to 

approximately 65%, allowing the line to reliably carry additional power during contingency 

conditions. This upgrade enhances reliability in the area and ensures secure power delivery for 

the 115 kV system.  

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

206 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

  PATENTGATE - PIONEER 345 KV LINE TAP345 KV (RELIABILITY/ECONOMIC) 

Figure 7.29: UMZ: Patentgate - Pioneer 345 kV Line Tap345 kV 

The 115 kV system to the southwest of Williston, North Dakota, experiences economic 
congestion, thermal overloads, and low voltage in the year five winter case and all year 10 cases. 
Kummer Ridge 345 kV also experiences low voltages during breaker faults at either Kummer 
Ridge or Round Up, resulting in the loss of either 345 kV branches between Charlie Creek and 
Kummer Ridge in the year five winter case. To address these needs, SPP recommends tapping 
the new Patentgate – Pioneer 345 kV line, issued in the 2024 ITP, and connecting it to Marley 
115 kV via two 345/115 kV transformers. This project provides an additional outlet for the 345 
kV system, raising voltage levels at Kummer Ridge, while also improving voltage throughout the 
local 115 kV system. The upgrade mitigates overloads and reduces congestion, providing 
significant relief across the 115 kV system, with congestion reductions ranging from 20% to 
100% depending on scenario and facility, and allowing the lower-voltage network to focus on 
local transfers rather than supporting long-distance 345 kV flows. This broad congestion relief 
supports more efficient power flows and improves operational flexibility across the local and 
regional network. By providing added capacity, enhanced voltage support, and reduced 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

207 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

congestion, this singular upgrade strengthens both local and regional reliability under multiple 
contingencies and operating conditions. 

  WHITE – AURORA – BMU NEW LINE 115 KV & TRANSFORMER 345/115 KV 

(PERSISTENT OPERATIONAL) 

Figure 7.30: UMZ: White - Aurora - BMU new line 115 kV & Transformer 345/115 kV 

An operational low-voltage exceedance was observed at Brookings, South Dakota, totaling four 
and a half days in the real-time models. This persistent condition stems from a load pocket in 
the Brookings area being served primarily by the local 115 kV network. To address this issue, 
SPP recommends adding a new 345/115 kV transformer at White, constructing a 115 kV line 
from BMU – Aurora – White, and closing the normally open 115 kV tie between two BMU 
Brookings stations. Together, these upgrades provide an additional inlet for power from the 345 
kV system, alleviating stress on the 115 kV network and restoring voltage levels. The project not 
only resolves the observed exceedance but also enhances operational reliability by ensuring the 
Brookings area can be supported under a wider range of conditions. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

208 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

  FT. THOMPSON TRANSFORMER 345/230 KV (ECONOMIC) 

Figure 7.31: UMZ: Ft. Thompson Transformer 345/230 kV 

At Fort Thompson, South Dakota, economic congestion occurs in both futures when either 
transformer at Fort Thompson or when the Grand Prairie – Holt County 345 kV line is lost. In 
these cases, the remaining transformer becomes congested, and the loss of the 345 kV line 
forces the 230 kV network to carry the bulk of transfer needs, further driving congestion on the 
transformers. To alleviate this, SPP recommends combining the two existing transformers and 
adding a new transformer as well, effectively uprating the current infrastructure. This upgrade 
eliminates all congestion by increasing transfer capability through the station, improving market 
efficiency, lowering production costs, and supporting more effective utilization of the regional 
transmission network. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

209 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

  SPRING BROOK – TANDE 345 KV, TAP AT WEST BANK SOUTH (RELIABILITY) 

Figure 7.32: UMZ: Spring Brook – Tande 345 kV, Tap at West Bank South Substation 345/115 kV 

The proposed new West Bank South 345/115 kV Substation, is a reliability-driven investment, 
from the 2024 ITP, for northwestern North Dakota. This project directly addresses 46 voltage 
violations in the 2025 ITP, significantly improving grid stability by providing another drop off 
point from the 345 kV. The most severe voltage violation occurs on the Farmvale 115 kV bus in 
year five winter season, following the loss of the Tioga – West Bank 115 kV circuit, a contingency 
that places unacceptable stress on the local system. 

SPP recommends accelerating this NTC from year 10 to year five to provide the necessary 
voltage support. By strengthening the existing HV backbone and introducing new 
transformation capability, the project supports long-term reliability, operational flexibility, and 
system resilience under contingency conditions. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

210 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

  HARRISBURG – DAYTON – LA VALLEY – VIRGIL FODNESS 115 KV REBUILD 

(ECONOMIC) 

Figure 7.33: UMZ: Harrisburg – Dayton 115 kV Rebuild 

In southeastern South Dakota, system congestion and reliability concerns are being addressed 
through targeted upgrades in key transmission paths, specifically involving flowgates in Future 1 
year five and Future 2 year five and 10. One critical constraint, La Valley – Virgil Fodness, for the 
loss of the 230 kV Sioux Falls – Virgil Fodness, has been identified as a bottleneck, limiting 
system performance and transfer capability in the region. 

The proposed upgrade — involving the Harrisburg – Dayton – LA Valley – Virgil Fodness 115 kV 
segments — directly relieves this constraint while also solving downstream congestion, which 
improves broader regional reliability and system efficiency. These upgrades will enhance power 
flow, reduce curtailments, and improve access to generation resources, supporting long-term 
planning goals and ensuring continued service reliability for customers. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

211 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

  BELFIELD – ROUNDUP 345 KV NEW LINE (ECONOMIC) 

Figure 7.34: UMZ: Belfield - Roundup 345 kV New Line 

The proposed Belfield – Roundup 345 kV transmission line solves year 10 economic congestion 
on the Belfield – Charlie Creek 345 kV line for the loss of the Dawson - Williston 230 kV line. 
Rebuilding the Belfield – Charlie Creek line is operationally infeasible due to outage constraints 
and substation limitations, with no viable reroute or temporary solution available. In contrast, 
Roundup has existing expansion capacity, and the new route presents fewer permitting and 
construction hurdles. This alternative improves long-term reliability, avoids outage risks, and 
aligns with SPP’s planning goals for flexibility and stakeholder engagement. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report

8 INFORMATIONAL PORTFOLIO 

ANALYSIS 

8.1  BENEFITS 

8.1.1  METHODOLOGY 

SPP used benefit metrics to measure the value and economic impacts of the consolidated 
portfolio. The Benefit Metrics Manual29 provides the definitions, concepts, calculations, and 
allocation methodologies for all approved metrics. The ESWG directed SPP staff to calculate the 
2025 ITP benefit-to-cost ratios for the final portfolio using the Future 1 and Future 2 models. 
The benefit analysis is performed on all reliability and economic projects in the consolidated 
portfolio. The benefit metrics listed below are calculated as the incremental benefit of the 
projects included in the entire portfolio as proposed by SPP Staff. 

Benefit Metrics: 

•  Adjusted Production Cost (APC) Savings 
•  Savings Due to Lower Ancillary Service Needs and Production Costs 
•  Avoided or Delayed Reliability Projects 
•  Marginal Energy Losses 
•  Capacity Cost Savings Due to Reduced On-Peak Transmission Losses 
•  Reduction of Emissions Rates and Values 
•  Public Policy Benefits 
•  Assumed Benefit of Mandated Reliability Projects 
•  Mitigation of Transmission Outage Costs 
• 

Increased Wheeling Through and Out Revenues 

8.1.2  APC SAVINGS 

APC captures the monetary cost associated with fuel prices, run times, grid congestion, unit 
operating costs, energy purchases, energy sales and other factors that directly relate to energy 
production by generating resources in the SPP footprint. Additional transmission projects aim to 
relieve system congestion and reduce costs through a combination of a more economical 
generation dispatch, more economical purchases and optimal revenue from sales. 

29 Benefit Metrics Manual 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

218 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

To calculate benefits over the expected 40-year life of the projects,30 SPP staff analyzed two 
years, 2029 and 2034. SPP staff calculated APC savings accordingly for these years, and then 
extrapolated the benefits for the initial five-year period based on the slope between the two 
points. After that, the benefits are assumed to grow at an inflation rate of 2.0% per year. SPP 
staff then discounted each year’s benefit to 2029 dollars using an 8% discount rate, and a 2.0% 
inflation rate from 2029 dollars back to 2025 dollars. The sum of all discounted benefits was 
presented as the PV benefit. SPP staff performed this calculation for every zone. 

Table 8.1 provides the zonal breakdown and the PV estimates of APC savings. Future 2 has 
higher congestion compared to Future 1. Therefore, the projects in the recommended portfolio 
provide more congestion relief in Future 2 than in Future 1, resulting in larger APC savings.

8.1.3  REDUCTION OF EMISSION RATES AND VALUES 

Additional transmission may result in a lower fossil-fuel burn (for example, less coal-intensive 
generation), resulting in less SO2, NOX, and CO2 emissions. Such a reduction in emissions is a 
benefit that is already monetized through the APC savings metric, based on the assumed 
allowance prices for these effluents. Note that neither ITP future assumes any allowance prices 
for CO2. 

8.1.4 SAVINGS DUE TO LOWER ANCILLARY SERVICE NEEDS AND 

PRODUCTION COSTS 

Ancillary services, such as spinning reserves, ramping (up/down), regulation, and 10-minute 
quick start are essential for the reliable operation of the electrical system. Additional 
transmission can decrease the ancillary services costs by: (a) reducing the ancillary services 
quantity needed, or (b) reducing the procurement costs for that quantity. 

The ancillary services needs in SPP are determined according to SPP’s market protocols and do 
not change based on transmission. Therefore, the savings associated with the “quantity” effect 
are assumed to be zero. 

The costs of providing ancillary services are captured in the APC metrics. The production cost 
simulations set aside the static levels of resources to provide regulation and spinning reserves. 
As a result, the benefits related to “procurement cost” effect are already included as a part of the 
APC savings presented in this report. 

8.1.5  AVOIDED OR DELAYED RELIABILITY PROJECTS 

SPP staff reviewed potential reliability needs to determine if the upgrades proposed for 
economic or policy reasons defer or replace any reliability upgrades. The avoided or delayed 
reliability project benefit represents the costs associated with these additional reliability 
upgrades that would otherwise have to be pursued.  

To calculate the avoided or delayed reliability project benefit for the recommended portfolio, 
SPP staff analyzed and identified the ability of economic projects to avoid or delay a reliability 
project in the optimization milestone. No overlap was identified; therefore, no avoided or 
delayed reliability projects were identified, and the associated benefits are estimated to be zero. 

8.1.6  CAPACITY COST SAVINGS DUE TO REDUCED ON-PEAK 

TRANSMISSION LOSSES  

Transmission line losses result from the interaction of line materials with the energy flowing over 
the line. This constitutes an inefficiency inherent to all standard conductors. Line losses across 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

220 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

the SPP system are directly related to system impedance. Transmission projects often reduce 
losses during peak load conditions, which lowers the costs associated with additional generation 
capacity needed to meet the capacity requirements. SPP staff calculated the capacity cost 
savings for the recommended portfolio based on the on-peak losses estimated in the BR 
powerflow model. SPP staff then multiplied the loss reductions by 112% to estimate the 
reduction in installed capacity requirements. The value of capacity savings is monetized by 
applying a net cost of new entry (CONE) of $85.61/kW-yr in 2018 dollars. The net CONE value 
was obtained from Attachment AA Resource Adequacy–Attachment AA Section 14 of the tariff. 
SPP assumed the net CONE to grow at an inflation rate of 2.0% for each study year, $36.6 million 
for 2029, and $79.3 million for 2034. Table 8.2 displays the associated capacity savings for each 
zone in each study year and the 40-year PV.

8.1.7  ASSUMED BENEFIT OF MANDATED RELIABILITY PROJECTS 

The assumed benefit of mandated reliability project is the metric that monetizes the benefits of 
reliability projects required to meet compliance and mitigate SPP criteria violations. The regional 
benefits are assumed to be equal to the 40-year PV of ATRRs of the projects, totaling $5,219 
million in 2025 dollars. 

The system reconfiguration approach to allocate zonal benefits utilizes the powerflow models to 
measure incremental flows shifted onto the existing system during an outage of the proposed 
reliability upgrade. SPP staff uses this as a proxy for how much each upgrade reduces flows on 
the existing transmission facilities in each zone. SPP staff used the results from the production 
cost simulations to determine hourly flow direction on the upgrades and applied as weighting 
factors for the powerflow results. 

Table 8.3 summarizes the system reconfiguration analysis results and the benefit allocation 
factors for different voltage levels. The table shows the overall zonal benefits calculated by 
applying these allocation factors.

8.1.8  BENEFIT FROM MEETING PUBLIC POLICY GOALS 

This metric represents the economic benefit provided by the transmission upgrades for 
facilitating public policy goals. In this study, the scope is limited to meeting public policy goals 
related to renewable energy. Systemwide benefits are assumed to be equal to the cost of policy 
projects.  

Since SPP staff identified no policy projects as a part of the recommended portfolio, the 
associated benefits are estimated to be zero. 

8.1.9  MITIGATION OF TRANSMISSION OUTAGE COSTS 

The standard production cost simulations used to estimate APC savings assume that 
transmission lines and facilities are available during all hours of the year, ignoring the added 
congestion-relief and production cost benefits of new transmission facilities during the planned 
and unplanned outages of existing transmission facilities. 

To estimate the incremental savings associated with the mitigation of transmission outage costs, 
the production cost simulations can be augmented for a realistic level of transmission outages. 
Due to the significant effort needed to develop these augmented models for each case, SPP 
used the findings from the RCAR II study to calculate this benefit metric for the consolidated 
portfolio as a part of this ITP Assessment. In the RCAR analysis, adding a subset of historical 
transmission outage events to the production cost simulations increased the APC savings by 
3.34%.31,32 Applying this ratio to the APC savings estimated for the recommended portfolio 
translates to a 40-year PV of benefits of $5,060.9 million for Future 1 and $8,300.6 million for 
Future 2 in 2025 dollars. These benefits are allocated based upon the load ratio share of the 
region. Table 8.4 shows the outage mitigation benefits allocated to each SPP zone.

8.1.10 INCREASED WHEELING THROUGH AND OUT REVENUES 

Due to the ESWG approved waiver on July 31, 2025, the Increased Wheeling Through and Out

8.1.11 MARGINAL ENERGY LOSSES BENEFIT 

The standard production cost simulations used to estimate APC do not reflect the impact of 
transmission upgrades on the MWh quantity of transmission losses. To make run-times more 
manageable, the load in the production cost simulations is “grossed up” for average 
transmission losses for each zone. These loss assumptions do not change with additional 
transmission. Therefore, the traditional APC metric does not capture the benefits from reduced 
MWh quantity of losses. 

APC savings due to such energy loss reductions can be estimated by post-processing the all 
components of the LMPs from simulation results and applying a methodology33 for marginal 
energy losses, which accounts for losses on generation and market imports. The 40-year PV of 

33 As described in the Benefit Metric Manual

Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

benefits is estimated to be $14.8 billion in Future 1 and $16.7 billion in Future, as shown in Table 
8.5 below.

The summary tables presented in section 8.1.12 will incorporate figures produced using this 
updated methodology to ensure consistency with the revised analytical approach. However, the 
figures shown below in Table 8.5 were calculated using the previous methodology and therefore 
may differ from results generated under the new process.

8.2  MARGINAL ENERGY LOSSES CALCULATION UPDATE 

After discussing this metric with the TWG and ESWG, SPP staff conducted additional review of 
the results and believe there is an error in how the metric is defined in the Benefit Metric 
Manual. 

This calculation has been sufficient for transmission portfolios in year’s past, however, this 
calculation deteriorates due to the extreme stress the model is under. The calculation becomes 
inaccurate when there are significant changes in transfers as it is considering the marginal loss 
changes of a zone being partially served by its own generation and partially served by 
importing. The previous calculation determines the loss factor based on the generation and load 
Marginal Energy Loss components divided by the LMP.  

SPP staff proposed a change to replace all instances where the calculation uses the full LMP 
component to only the Marginal Energy Cost component of the LMP to be consistent with the 
SPP Integrated Marketplace Protocols. Using the full LMP in accelerated load and generation 
scenarios may lead to double counting congestion costs that are not related to transmission 
losses. The proposed calculation determines the loss factor by dividing the generation and load 
Marginal Loss Components by the Marginal Energy Cost component to determine the projected 
loss factor.  

SPP staff has obtained approval from the ESWG to implement the new calculation methodology 
within the Benefit Metrics Manual for future studies. The results below have been updated and 
are based on the calculations that are consistent with the SPP Integrated Market Protocols. 

The figures in Table 8.10 below were calculated on the full portfolio.

8.3  RATE IMPACTS35 

The rate impact to an average retail residential ratepayer in SPP was computed for the complete 
recommended portfolio. Rate impact costs36 and benefits37 are allocated to the average retail 
residential ratepayer based on an estimated residential consumption of 1,000 kilowatt hours 
(kWh) per month. Benefits and costs for the 2034 study year were used to calculate rate impacts. 
All 2034 benefits and costs are shown in 2025 dollars, discounting at a 2.0% inflation rate.  

The retail residential rate impact benefit is subtracted from the retail residential rate impact cost 
to obtain a net rate impact cost by zone. If the net rate impact cost is negative, it indicates a net 
benefit to the zone. The rate impact costs and benefits are shown in Table 8.11 through Table 
8.14. There is a monthly net benefit for the average SPP residential ratepayer of $17.25 for 
Future 1. There is a monthly net benefit for the average SPP residential ratepayer of $32.37 for 
Future 2. Table 8.11 through Table 8.14 shows the rate impact to an average retail residential 
ratepayer in SPP based on the previous 12-month historical consumption. The figures below 
were calculated on the full portfolio as proposed by SPP Staff.

8.3.1  RATE IMPACT CALCULATION IMPROVEMENTS 

A load ratio share (LRS) value is used to support rate impacts calculations. To determine this 
value (also known as 12-CP), staff averages the LRS from each of the last 12 monthly coincident 
peaks recorded in real-time operations. Thus, this value is based upon historical load ratios.  

The use of this value makes sense during periods of stagnant or consistent load growth across 
the region, however, as noted many times in this report, SPP is experiencing rapid load growth 
inconsistently across the footprint. Based upon this knowledge, using a more forward-looking 
calculation approach for the LRS value is more appropriate.  

To calculate a more reasonable LRS value, staff utilized the 2025 ITP load forecast data and 
futures assumptions, leading to different LRS values for each future. LRSF1 is based solely on the 
Base Reliability model peak demand forecast provided by SPP members. LRSF2 includes the same 
load forecast as Future 1 but also includes the peak load values for the spot loads added via the 
resiliency conditions.  

Using different LRS values for each future, will affect the Rate Impact-Cost Column in the Rate 
Impact Tables. The following 4 tables38 reflect the updated values for zonal and state rate 
impacts for each future based upon the more forward-looking 2025 ITP LRS methodology.  

38 As noted above rate impacts will be updated as necessary based upon the final portfolio and an 
endorsed for construction portfolio and approved project need dates.

8.4  BENEFITS & RATE IMPACTS (BOARD APPROVED) 

The Board approved a portion of the staff-recommended portfolio. This decision reflects a 
targeted adoption of the projects and measures that were determined to strike the appropriate 
balance of reliability and affordability at this stage. A detailed summary of the resulting benefit 
metrics, along with the associated rate impacts for stakeholders, is provided in sections 8.4.1 and 
8.4.2 below.

8.5  VOLTAGE STABILITY ASSESSMENT 

A voltage stability assessment was conducted with the recommended portfolio using Future 1 
and 2 RPM models to assess the transfer limit (GW) from each of the six LOLE zones to the rest 
of the SPP footprint. The assessment was performed to determine whether the generation 
dispatch with the recommended portfolios adversely impacts system voltage stability. The 
assessment was intentionally scoped to determine how the planned system performs under 
differing dispatches based on the six LOLE zones. 

8.5.1  METHODOLOGY 

To determine the amount of generation transfer that could be accommodated by the planned 
system, generation in the source zone was increased and generation in the sink zone was 
decreased. The Transfer Zones were defined as all generation located within the six LOLE Zones 
individually. 

The following Transfers (Table 8.28) were performed on the 2029 Summer, 2029 Winter, 2034 
Summer, 2034 Winter, Future 1 and Future 2 models. Additional analysis was also performed 
with the source and sink LOLE Zones swapped from what is shown in the table to ensure that no 
relevant transfer limits were missed due to the initial dispatch in the models. 

Source LOLE 
Zone(s) 

Sink LOLE Zone(s) 

1 

2 
3 
4 
5 

6 

2,3,4,5,6 

1,3,4,5,6 
1,2,4,5,6 
1,2,3,5,6 
1,2,3,4,6 

1,2,3,4,5 

Table 8.28: LOLE Zones 

Single contingencies (N-1) for all SPP branches, transformers, and ties greater than or equal to 
345 kV were analyzed. SPP and first-tier 100 kV and above facilities were monitored for voltage 
and thermal violations. The initial condition for each model was the source zone sum of real 
power generation output (MW). The maximum source zone transfer capability was the sum of 
the LOLE Zones real power maximum generation (Pmax). Transfer analysis was performed on 
each model in 50 MW steps until voltage collapse occurred in the pre-contingency and post-
contingency (N-1, 345 kV, 500 kV and 765 kV facilities) conditions. Each future was evaluated for 
increasing generation transfer amounts to determine different voltage collapse points of the 
transmission system. Source and sink generation was scaled on a pro-rata basis to reach the 
pre-contingency maximum power transfer limit, or the voltage stability limit (VSL). Transfer limits 
were determined based on the worst N-1 contingency and independently evaluating the next 
worst contingency to determine the most impactful contingency to VSL. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

250 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

8.5.2 SUMMARY 

Table 8.29 shows a summary of the voltage stability assessment limits by future, model and 
transfer path. The table includes the transfer path, source and sink generation pre-transfer 
levels, critical contingency, post transfer level when VSL is reached, and whether or not thermal 
overloads occur prior to voltage collapse. The table shows in all instances either the minimum 
internal generation levels were reached or when a thermal limit was reached before the VSL.

8.5.3 CONCLUSION 

The analysis demonstrates the planned system does not reach a VSL prior to system thermal 
limits in the summer peak or winter models; therefore, the potential benefits attributed to the 
consolidated portfolio are validated. Voltage collapse occurs, in many scenarios, at generation 
levels less than the projected capacity amounts. However, thermal issues (i.e., justification for 
curtailments) precede collapse. The APC benefit of the consolidated portfolio is generally 
derived from relieving congestion on thermal issues. Many transfers reached the minimum 
source or sink generation limit which further demonstrates the benefit of the portfolio by 
showing a large degree of flexibility in generation dispatch between LOLE Zones prior to 
reaching stability limits. 

8.6  FINAL RELIABILITY ASSESSMENT 

SPP staff incorporated all projects from the 2025 ITP consolidated portfolio, along with model 
adjustments identified during solution development, into the BR and short-circuit models. A 
contingency analysis consistent with the scope and procedures outlined in sections 4.2.1 and 
4.2.2 of the ITP Manual was then performed to evaluate whether the selected projects 
introduced any new reliability violations. The objective is to ensure that potential adverse 
outcomes are recognized and that an appropriate level of system reinforcement is identified to 
maintain overall system performance. 

Uncertainty in the timing and magnitude of future load additions, particularly due to deferments 

in load interconnection, combined with additional uncertainty around the level of reactive 

support required on 765 kV facilities, has led to a deferment of project selection for this 

assessment. SPP will continue to monitor areas of concern under these conditions and reassess 

potential system reinforcements in future cycles as more information on load development 

becomes available. 

8.6.1  SHORT-CIRCUIT MODEL  

A proxy automatic sequencing fault calculation (ASCC) short-circuit analysis was performed on 

the 2025 ITP year-two summer maximum fault current model to find percent increases in fault 

currents in relation to the base case model on which the needs assessment was performed. SPP 

staff added all consolidated portfolio projects expected to alter or need zero sequence data to 

the model regardless of their in-service dates. After performing this analysis, SPP staff found 

that 33 of the 11,409 buses monitored experienced a 5% increase in fault current

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

260 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

8.7  SENSITIVITY ANALYSIS 

To evaluate the robustness of market outcomes under a range of future uncertainties, a 
comprehensive economic sensitivity analysis was conducted for the SPP RTO service area. This 
analysis is intended to test the performance of the selected transmission portfolio across a 
spectrum of plausible future conditions, reflecting variability in key market drivers such as fuel 
prices, load growth, renewable energy development, hedging behavior, and federal policy 
support. 

The primary objective of this sensitivity analysis is twofold: 

•  To assess the impacts of external uncertainties on system-wide economic metrics, 

such as APC, energy prices, congestion patterns, and generation dispatch; and 

•  To stress test the transmission portfolio to ensure it continues to provide economic 
value and system benefits across a broad range of future scenarios. The sensitivities 
examined in this analysis represent credible deviations from the base case, grounded in 
data from authoritative sources such as the U.S. Energy Information Administration’s 
2025 Annual Energy Outlook (AEO), recent federal legislation, and stakeholder inputs 
through the SPP ESWG. Each sensitivity was modeled independently to isolate the effect 
of a single driver, with select sensitivities combined where interaction effects were 
considered relevant. 

These modeled sensitivities include variations in: 

•  Natural gas prices (High / Low) 
•  Energy demand growth (High / Low) 
•  Wind energy penetration (High / Low) 
•  Solar energy penetration (High / Low) 
•  Hedging strategies, shifting from perfect hedging to a 70% hedging assumption 
•  Federal production tax credits, specifically the removal of Production Tax Credit (PTCs) 

for qualifying renewable technologies 

The transmission portfolio was evaluated against each of these cases to determine its ability to 
maintain or enhance system benefits under stress. Overall, the portfolio demonstrated a high 
degree of resilience, continuing to show net economic benefits and mitigating adverse market 
outcomes in nearly all sensitivity scenarios. 

This level of robustness underscores the strategic value of the selected projects, not only under 
expected conditions but also in more extreme or divergent futures. The upcoming sections 
present a detailed comparison of key market outcomes across the base case and sensitivity 
scenarios, along with a discussion of how the portfolio’s performance varied under each stress 
test.

the percentage changes from the 2025 EIA AEO High and Low Economic Growth cases relative

than twice the amount of the increase, as modeled in the low-demand case.

Figure 8.8: Removal of PTC 

Figure 8.8 presents the B/C ratios of the portfolio under a sensitivity that mimics the removal of 
PTCs for renewables. Similar to the wind sensitivity, this scenario showed that the portfolio 
delivers the highest benefits under low-wind conditions, which align with the assumption of PTC 
removal. Overall, this sensitivity suggests that the portfolio would continue to perform well even 
in the absence of renewable tax incentives. 

Figure 8.9: 40 Year APC B/C Ranges 

Figure 8.9 presents the 40-year APC benefit ranges, highlighting the maximum and minimum 
APC benefits observed across all sensitivity scenarios. The chart also displays three reference 
cost levels at the bottom left of the graph: (1) the total portfolio cost, which includes both 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

266 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

reliability and economic projects; (2) the economic-only portfolio cost; and (3) an alternative 
estimate of the full portfolio cost assuming a 1.5 mileage modifier factor, rather than a 1.25 
modifier, for the 765 kV components of the portfolio. Each of these cost estimates includes a 
±30% range to reflect potential variability. 

Notably, even under the most severe demand reduction scenario, the estimated APC benefits 
still greatly exceed all three cost estimates, reinforcing the overall value of the portfolio under a 
wide range of assumptions. Additionally, the cumulative increase in APC benefits across the 
more favorable sensitivity scenarios is significantly greater than the cumulative reduction 
observed in the less favorable ones. This asymmetry—visible in the tornado chart as a larger 
sum of benefits on the right side compared to the left—further demonstrates the resilience and 
upside potential of the portfolio. 

2025 ITP Assessment Report 

267 

 
 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 

9 NTC RECOMMENDATIONS 

SPP makes NTC recommendations for projects included in the consolidated portfolio based on 
results from the staging process and SPP Business Practice 7060. If financial expenditure is 
required within five years from Board approval, the project is generally recommended by SPP 
staff for an NTC. To determine the date when financial expenditure is required, SPP staff 
subtracts the project’s lead time from its need date. Expected lead times for transmission 
projects are determined using historical data on construction timelines from SPP’s project 
tracking process. Table 9.1 below shows SPP’s NTC recommendations when considering staging 
results, expected lead times and other qualitative information related to the recommended 
projects. 

SPP staff plans to bring a revision request to extend the financial commitment window from four 
years to five years. NTC issuance recommendations have been updated to assume this revision 
request is accepted. If it is rejected, the previous four year financial commitment window will still 
be in effect and some projects may not receive NTC’s as designated in the table below.

Table 9.1: 2025 ITP NTC Recommendations 

During portfolio development for the 2025 ITP, SPS submitted a data revision request for the 2026 
ITP model series which reduced their load forecast for the year two and year five models. Based 
on these reductions, SPP staff made the decision to withhold issuing NTCs for specific projects 
that  might  no  longer  be  immediately  necessary  based  on  the  shifted  timeframe  for  the  load 
increase in New Mexico. These projects have been staged according to 2025 ITP data and remain 
as part of the portfolio, but will not all receive NTCs from this study.
